## Global Data Watch

0 After a strong 1H24, softer data is still consistent with 2H24 moderation

• Fiscal drags to temper EMU recovery, smoothing way to ECB cuts

0 China data mixed but on net soft, tracking weak 3.5%ar 2Q growth

Up next: US real PCE (0.4%), Core PCE (0.14%); Riksbank hold

## Let’s take a step back

A key call in our global outlook for this year has been that the expansion would gradually slow under the pressure of sticky inflation that kept financial conditions tight. In the event, inflation has proven even stickier than we had expected, leading to a significant delay in the anticipated easing cycles of central banks (Figure 1). Broader financial conditions have taken this repricing in stride and not overreacted, but they also remain a damping force on activity. Despite this, global GDP is still on track to advance 2.7%ar in 1H24, nearly half a percentage point stronger than potential and even stronger than our 2.1%ar forecast at the start of the year. It is against this backdrop of ongoing resilience in the expansion (and continued expectations for a moderation) that the latest soft-patch in the data needs to be viewed against. On balance, we see the data as consistent with our outlook for global GDP to decelerate in the coming quarter but also expand at a trend-like pace of 2.2%ar in 2H24.

The latest data have been mixed, but are challenging what earlier this quarter had looked like a broadening in the global expansion across regions and sectors with potential upside risks. Our confidence in a global manufacturing pickup was bolstered earlier this year as a large inventory drag was likely to reverse in the face of strong gains in final goods demand (Figure 2). In the event, global industry accelerated through spring, with output expanding 1.7%(3m3m, saar) through April and stronger May gains reported this week from the US and China. At the same time, we reported this week that global real retail sales contracted in April for a third straight month and is down 0.4%ar on a %3m3m basis. In the US, downward revisions to retail sales in March and April reinforce the global soft patch.

We remain confident, however, that the global consumer will perk back up given healthy fundamentals, including strong job growth, solid wage gains, softening inflation and robust wealth-effects. A solid 0.4% gain in US retail control spending

Japan: A return to positive interest rates – midyear 2024 outlook

for May along with a stronger-than-expected 1.4% jump in China real retail spending suggest these supports are helping. Continued signs of an acceleration in global capex add to the sense that global final goods demand should bounce back and support the lift in global industry.

Business surveys are the leading edge of tracking the global economy, and we have taken considerable signal in seeing the monthly PMIs advance from their lows last November. The May PMIs were particularly encouraging, showing improvements in both manufacturing and services as well as an anticipated rebound in Europe. However, the drop across Europe and Japan in this week’s June flash readings is disappointing. While the US bucked the trend and posted a modest rise, our composite DM flash PMI slipped 0.7-point in June (Figure 3), with a 1.5-point drop in the manufacturing component challenging our projected pickup in global industry.

![](images/3eec1080813b76411e35c59a9858ad5d40cfb5f1e1d80a317bd4215b67f21570.jpg)

Amid the step back in the consumer goods spending and survey data, it is also important to step back to get perspective. Data are always noisy, and so we are not over-interpreting the recent declines in the PMI, particularly after seven straight monthly gains. But it also important to not ignore signals. On balance, we are inclined to fade the recent monthly moves in the PMI and rely on the trend and also recognize the healthy fundamental supports for spending growth. Moreover, a levelling out in the trend is consistent with our forecast for a downshift in growth in 2H24. At 52.6, the all-industry DM flash PMI suggests 1.1%ar growth in real GDP. Although this is below our forecast for a 1.7%ar gain this quarter, the flash reading is aligned our call for 3Q24 (Figure 3).

## EMU threading the needle

The decline in the Euro area flash June PMI raises some concern that the nascent rebound is being cut short. The abruptness of the drop is notable against the backdrop of the French election, which was mentioned explicitly by firms as a reason for the drag. But a large decline in Germany as well is a reminder that structural headwinds remain. Still, after surprising to the upside in 1H24, we maintain that GDP will decelerate to a 1-1.5%ar pace in 2H24. The 2Q24 average of the PMI is consistent with our forecast for 1.5%ar GDP growth this quarter, while the June reading is aligned with the lower end of this range. Fiscal drags are reinforcing this cautious out-

look. The European Commission has proposed putting five EMU countries into excessive deficit procedures (EDP), including France and Italy. This requires them to improve their primary structural deficits by 0.5% per year. This is already embedded in our outlook. However, such a program may be challenged by national governments, including in France, testing the credibility of EU rules.

The sluggishness of Euro area growth should smooth the path for ECB cuts later this year, which is being pressured by elevated price and wage inflation. Although details on the May inflation report show that as much as 0.2%-pt of the 0.58%m/ m gain in regional service prices last month is due to holidayeffects, the remaining pace is still elevated. Falling core goods prices are a partial offset, but signs of some cooling in services prices will be important if the ECB is to cut rates again in September and December. Wages are also a concern but moderating. The 7% pay demand from IG Metall this week will likely be seen as constructive, given that past deals have tended to be half of the demand.

## A dove-hawk divide through the North Sea

The BoE kept rates on hold this week, but some dovish elements of the statement suggest it is heading for an August rate cut provided there are no further upside inflation surprises. The upside surprise in the May CPI services print this week was quite narrow, with most of the subcomponents well behaved. At the same time, a strong retail sales gain and rising consumer confidence point to some upside risk around 2Q GDP, but the signal for 3Q is tempered by a decline in the June PMI. Meanwhile, opinion polls suggest Labour is on track for a very significant majority, and attention is turning to how fiscal policies evolve under constraints.

In contrast to the dovish tone from the BoE, the Norges Bank pushed back against a near-term easing, changing forward guidance from a cut in September to one in 1Q25. The move, which was motivated by stronger domestic demand and higher wage growth, was more hawkish than general expectations. While we stick to our call of a first cut in December, the decision once again demonstrates Norges Bank’s status as a hawkish outlier in Western Europe. In contrast, the Riksbank is expected at next week’s meeting to guide for two more cuts in 2H24. We expect the Board to consider the record-high services price increase in May stemming from three Taylor Swif concerts as temporary.

## BoJ sets the table

The BoJ’s decision last week to wait until July to announce its QT roadmap was less of a reaction to incoming data weakness, and more an attempt to give the market time to digest the next important shift in policy that is now in the cards.

Activity indicators through 2Q are painting a varied picture of demand, with a surprise fall in the June services PMI implying activity stalled this month. By again stating that a hike is possible, Ueda’s comments this week aim to leave all options on the table for next month, particularly in the face of a renewed yen slide. Between now and the July meeting, the 2Q Tankan survey released July 1 will be closely watched, as will incoming inflation and consumption data. But we think it would be a mistake to put too much weight on the high-frequency data flow in judging the BoJ’s upcoming policy path. By removing YCC and negative rates, the BoJ has set the stage for a “substantial” reduction in bond purchases and signaled that hikes above the zero bound are coming.

## China: Continued soft data in May

China’s activity data improved in May but were on net a bit softer than expected, leaving GDP on track for a weak 3.5%ar gain this quarter. IP disappointed with a modest 0.4%m/m gain, only partially reversing its March-April decline. Consumers meanwhile perked up a bit, with retail sales up 1.5%m/m while services activity also ticked higher. Industrial policy supports continue to fuel gains in manufacturing fixed asset investment, but the housing sector remains a large drag. House prices, home sales, starts, and real estate investment also recorded steepening declines last month, suggesting that housing policy relaxation has not been effective in restoring market demand. Despite the step up in government bond issuance, bank lending slowed to a new historical low in May and we revised down our TSF forecast to 8.5% from 9%. The PBOC’s caution in easing monetary policy (we now look for just one 10bp policy rate cut this year from two previously) keeps pressure on fiscal policy to deliver our forecast of trend-like growth (4.5%) in 2H24. The risk is that, with real estate drags continuing and private sector investment growth lackluster, policies narrowly geared toward strategic industries and credit will be insufficient for growth.

![](images/b329f4f6959fcacc44ccf45fe3858cd675f2e3abb17d3ea1fac41bf31f817fb6.jpg)

## Robust Asia exports, with some hiccups

Despite the expected 2Q slowing in EM Asia GDP growth, signals from North Asia’s bellwethers are encouraging for both regional GDP growth and the global capex cycle. May exports moderated across the region, but prior strength has helped sustained its uptrend. Taiwan’s export orders posted a solid gain buoyed by tech, but the uptrend looks to be broadening out from AI-related products to other traditional tech products as the global tech inventory correction comes to an end. The pick-up in machinery orders is also encouraging for the upturn we are tracking in global capex. Evidence of improvement in non-tech exports remains more mixed across the region. Korea’s early June export data points to a resumption of robust 2Q export growth after last quarter’s -0.8%ar contraction. But Japan’s export volumes fell in May— dragged down by weaker auto shipments—and April core machinery orders also declined sharply. The risk in Japan is that a sustained auto sector drag—owing to temporary output suspension amid an ongoing safety testing scandal—also weighs on output, exports, and sentiment into early 3Q.

## CEE easing running out of steam

Central Europe’s easing cycles are running out of steam as disinflation progress has slowed and DM central banks appear unlikely to deliver material near-term easing. The NBH this week slowed the pace of its cuts to 25bp (from 50bp) and delivered a hawkish message. Despite leaving the door open to further cuts, the bank mentioned for the first time the possibility of a pause in upcoming meetings. We look for the Czech National Bank to downshift to a 25bp easing pace at next week’s meeting, although 50bp is possible. Core inflation eased in the May print, but elevated services inflation has kept inflation above target, wage growth has surprised to the upside and consumption is picking up. We expect the NBH and CNB to ease a further 100bp in 2H24, but this call is contingent on inflation, currencies, as well as Fed/ECB easing.

## Politics curtails LatAm easing

While Chile’s CBC delivered a 25bp cut this week and Colombia’s BanRep is expected to cut 50bp next week, concerns over political and policy risks in Brazil and Mexico are limiting central banks’ space for easing. In Brazil, the change in the 2025 fiscal target, questions over the sustainability of the fiscal framework, and political divisions on the COPOM have soured appetite for Brazilian assets and raised inflation expectations. This backdrop – combined the solid growth and a tight labor market – resulted in the BCB interrupting the easing cycle at 10.5% this week. The unanimous decision to hold rates tempers some concern over political division on the board, and an extended hold (our base case) should help to contain the rise of inflation expectations. In Mexico, the material sell-off in the peso following Morena’s uncomfortably strong victory, along with still-sticky services inflation, limits Banxico’s room to maneuver. We see the next cut coming only in September with just 50bp of easing this year. This week’s partial cabinet announcements were positive from an economic perspective, but do not change our view on longerterm institutional risks.

For additional detail, see our full policy rate QED report.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 2Q24 global real GDP growth was unchanged this week at 2.2%ar (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster ticked down 0.1%-pt to 2.3%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth (Table 2).

Almost half of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in just over half of these economies (Figure 2).

• Our CapexNow model suggests an improvement in capex growth from 2.4%ar in 1Q24 to 3.2%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

Momentum in global consumer goods spending looks to have slowed further in April, although the fundamentals for an anticipated regional rebalancing remain intact. While global factory output declined for a second consecutive month in April, three-month trend growth is still running at a solid 2.5%ar.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds sold off modestly over the past week amid somewhat softer economic data, as the previous flight-to-quality flow was partially unwound. In the US, yields remain close to the lower end of their recent ranges and valuations in 5Y remain rich relative to 2Y and 10Y. We keep tactical shorts in 5Y USTs, though with somewhat reduced conviction given the expected strength in May retail sales data did not materialize and event risk from the first round of the French election on Jun 30<sup>th</sup> is approaching. We also keep 5s/30s steepeners.

In the Euro area, spreads have stabilized over the past week after the previous widening after the snap French election was called. We retain a bullish bias on duration and would look to add longs if 5-10Y German yields rise closer to 2.6%. We hold 5s/10s Germany and 2Yx2Y/10Yx10Y EUR swaps steepeners. In the UK, the BoE kept rates on hold as expected with a 7-2 vote, though the minutes had a dovish tilt in tone. We continue to expect a 25bp cut at the Aug and Nov meetings, and have a bias for a flatter Aug/Nov24 MPC OIS curve. We hold tactical 10s/30s flatteners.

In EM, some of the political uncertainty in South Africa has faded after a Government of National Unity was agreed, but we are reluctant to chase the rally and stay neutral, though shift to a bullish bias. In China, some additional scope for PBoC easing support keeps us bullish duration, but only on a currency-hedged basis. Overall, we remain neutral duration overall, with OWs Poland, Türkiye and Uruguay offset by a short duration overlay and an UW in Thailand.

## Credit

Leveraged loan credit metrics are showing signs of stabilizing, but a large gap exists between public and private company balance sheets. Notably, last quarter we saw a decline in leverage for the 11th time over the last 12 quarters, but a gap between public (4.5x) and private companies (5.7x); revenues and EBITDA expanded y/y at the third slowest and slowest rates over the last 3Y, respectively; interest expense decreased q/q; and interest coverage rose slightly overall but public (4.15x) coverage ratios are more than twice that for private companies (2.04x) (1Q24 Leveraged Loan Credit Fundamentals, Jun 18<sup>th</sup>).

European credit markets broadly stabilized this week, with IG and HY 1bp and 10bp tighter, respectively. The preference in our conversations with clients so far has been for French international corporates with significant overseas revenue over purely domestic issuers. We have been recommending to buy ‘dislocations’, such as Italian bank AT1 and to a lesser extent short-call hybrids, although these have already partially retraced. In terms of valuations, French HG issuers only trade marginally wide to broader credit indices after the recent moves. For non-financial corporates, this is the continuation of a medium-term trend which has seen aggregate French spreads move from 10-15bp tight to the index in 2019, to essentially trading in-line prior to this latest move. Our European Credit Machine Learning model has remained long risk throughout this volatility episode so far (European Credit Weekly, Jun 21<sup>st</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/add6964fc456b9e9422fa9d5bb917c21cee38d11f4587d0a17b408fef388aa5b.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

The investment opportunities from the data center dynamic should last for years. That is important to remember in the context of credit markets sitting at or near-multi year tights. Many of these names and securities should benefit from these underlying dynamics even through the vicissitudes of the normal credit market cycle. We like them now, and will probably like them even more down the road. The effective bid-ask on US data center growth is 3-13 GW per annum, and we have gravitated to 8GW per annum due to the very real constraints on growth across power, energy, water, copper, construction capacity, and perhaps most importantly regulatory constraints. But even through our skeptical lens and under what we think are realistic assumptions, there is an enormous amount of growth in the data center universe over the medium term with structural implications across sectors and asset classes (US Credit Research, Jun 17<sup>th</sup>).

## Currencies

Does the dollar smile still exist? USD has strengthened in periods of both US growth exceptionalism and moderation in 2024, leading some to question if the dollar smile still even exists. Our answer is YES, but with caveats, that it will be narrow and that the composition of USD strength will be different on either ends of the smile. On one end, US exceptionalism like in 1Q will weigh on funders while a shift to the defensive part of the smile will weigh more on high yielders. The valley, though narrow, is best played through the midyielders (Scandis, AUD in G10).

Trade Recommendations: Initiated short EUR/NOK, long NOK/SEK & long AUD/NZD last week. Rotate AUD vs. JPY & CHF basket into long AUD/JPY cash. Keep USD longs vs. EUR & CNH in options. Stay MW EM FX into H2 due to US election risk and unresolved macro uncertainties (FXMW, Jun 21<sup>st</sup>).

## Commodities

We believe the recent pullback in commodities is just that—a pullback—and we continue to see a 10% appreciation in the broader BCOM Commodities index by year-end. Given the latest sell-off, agricultural commodities like sugar and wheat move to the top of our list of preferred longs, with a projected 30% and 25% return, respectively, by year-end. Brent oil is back on the long trade list with an unchanged target of \$90 by September. We remain structurally bullish on gold and silver and see the ongoing consolidation as a buying opportunity with a target of \$2,600/oz and \$34/oz, respectively, in 2025. We expected a price correction in copper and feel current levels present a good re-entry point towards our \$11,500 target by 3Q25. Lastly, after rallying \~12%, US HH natural gas moves to the bottom of the list for now (Got Commodities this summer?, Jun 20<sup>th</sup>).

Global oil demand expanded by 1.4 mbd month-to-date in June over year ago levels, supported by rising US gasoline consumption and robust summer travel activity in Europe and Asia. Gasoline demand in the US has been on a steady rise since the Memorial Day weekend and we expect a further advance as a record 71 million Americans are expected to travel during the upcoming July 4<sup>th</sup> holiday (Oil Demand & Inventory Tracker, Jun 20<sup>th</sup>).

Hot weather expected to push June to the warmest on record since 1950 will likely raise total natural gas power burn for June to \~41 Bcf/day. This would mark the highest natural gas power burn level for June. As wind and solar have tempered US natural gas power demand strength, structural demand growth in electricity load appears to be providing a soft floor for US natural gas power burn. We hold our bullish price call for 2024 in place with the balance of 2024 expected to average \$3.50/MMBtu, and revise our 2025 price forecast lower to \$4.30/MMBtu (Power burn perseveres in the face of wind, Jun 21<sup>st</sup>).

## The pushback on policy to limit climate change

• COP28 acknowledged the world was not on track to meet the Paris objectives, urging new efforts

• Ahead of COP29 there is increased pushback against pursuing climate aims aggressively

• Meanwhile, data show the run of record global average temperatures has now extended to 12 months

Scientists and policymakers commonly describe the actions taken during this decade as critical if the objectives of the 2015 Paris climate agreement are to be met (the objectives are to keep man-made climate change well below 2 degrees C, with efforts to limit it to 1.5C). The first “Global Stock Take”, undertaken ahead of and presented at COP28, concluded that the world is not on track to meet those objectives (Figure 1). COP28 restated the ambition to meet the Paris objectives, while formally recognizing the need to transition away from fossil fuels for the first time. But there is growing scepticism over two things.

First is whether plans developed at national level (nationally determined contributions or NDCs) will deliver sufficient reductions in greenhouse gas emissions at a global level to limit climate change. And second is whether individual countries will take the action required to deliver on their individual plans.

Figure 1: Greenhouse Gas Emissions scenarios GT, CO2 equivalent per year
![](images/c632c0cca80e486e835096cb113b785b01a5fe81f40f5d3c047ba45412c8c6b6.jpg)

COP29 is to be held in Baku, Azerbaijan, starting November 11. Without the impulse created by the stocktake exercise, expectations that it will create a step change in efforts to combat climate change are not high. There is more focus on COP30, which will take place in 2025 in Brazil, and which should have in hand a full new set of NDCs covering at least the period up to 2035. The proof of whether the COP process is delivering will be in the extent to which those NDCs show a more rapid decline in emissions, alongside credible means of implementing those declines. At this time writing there are not many grounds for thinking this will be the case. Indeed,

recent months have seen pushback against efforts to limit climate change become more evident in a number of ways:

Rising support for the political right in Europe. While the EU has long positioned itself as a leader on climate change efforts, it has become increasingly common for parties on the political right to question the speed and necessity of its environmental policy. This has already forced portions of the EU’s “Green Deal” package to be watered down. The situation is far from uniform across Europe - in the UK, for example, current polling indicates that a Conservative Party which has watered down is climate commitments looks set to lose an election to a Labour opposition more committed to net zero. But amid gains for the political right in recent EU parliamentary elections, and likely in the French election, based on current polling, the prior consensus around environmental policy is being challenged.

Drill, baby, drill. During his Presidency, Mr Trump withdrew from the Paris agreement, an action reversed by his successor. While the views of the prospective Republican nominee on climate change are well known, his support for more drilling for fossil fuels has been very explicit this time around, with reports that he has explicitly sought campaign funding from the CEOs of oil companies. With the divergence between Republican and Democrat attitudes on climate policy widening, US policy and engagement with the Paris process will depend on the outcome of the Presidential election.

Trade tensions on EVs. China has prioritized industries associated with the green transition as part of its multiyear strategic policy, and has established market leadership positions in a number of related sectors, including batteries and electric vehicles (EVs). The EU has announced that it will impose tariffs on imports of EVs from Chinese manufacturers on the grounds that they have benefited from unfair state support compared to EU producers. Note that in the US Inflation Reduction Act, subsidies and incentives for green transition are often linked to value-added being produced within the US. The lingering theme is that major industrial nations are sensitive to ensuring that they secure jobs and activity within their own borders as the green transition occurs.

With an election due by late September 2025, Australian opposition leader Peter Dutton has stated his centre-right Coalition would scrap Australia’s commitment to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 43% (compared to 2005 levels) by 2030 should they be elected. Although the Coalition would retain the target to reach net zero by 2050, they propose a shift to nuclear power generation (as opposed to solar and wind), which we note will take longer to deliver emissions reductions.

## Little progress at Bonn pre-COP 29

Negotiators met in Bonn over the first two weeks of June to prepare for COP29. As acknowledged by UN organisers, however, progress has been limited with “too many items are still on the table . . . We’ve left ourselves with a very steep mountain to climb to achieve ambitious outcomes in Baku”. The sticking point issues are familiar and inter-related ones.

First is how to ensure that financial flows from developed to developing economies are large enough to allow the latter to both transition to green energy sources and production, and to mitigate climate effects already in train. How large those flows should be, what form they should take (grants versus loans), and exactly who should be contributors and recipients remain contentious. A second set of issues revolves around making sure that NDCs are collectively ambitious enough and progress toward them is verifiable. For many developing countries, the issues of funding versus the scale of climate ambition are inextricably linked.

## Run of monthly heat records continues

Warnings of the likely failure of the efforts to keep man-made temperature change below 1.5 degrees C have not generated a step change in efforts to limit emissions. At the same time, measured climate outcomes are reinforcing the evidence that human activity has caused weather systems to change and average temperatures to rise.

According to the EU’s Copernicus Climate Change Service, May 2024 saw warmer average surface air temperatures than any other month on record, the 12th month in a row to hit such a record. While May was 1.52C above the pre-industrial average, temperatures over the last twelve months have averaged 1.63C above that average. Global sea surface temperatures have seen a record for the last 14 months. Although a similar streak of record monthly temperatures was seen over 2015-16, the extent by which prior records have been exceeded is larger this time around (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Global surface temperature increase over pre-industrial Degrees C, yellow line is 12 month trailing average
![](images/892301fdd8a9cd08b1fa82fdb22fd498a1d1810ed0fffec087190c80c6e35177.jpg)

The World Meteorological Organisation and other forecasters expect the El Niño conditions which have contributed to recent records to fade in the second half of 2024, suggesting the run of recent monthly records should end. But few climatologists doubt that recent outcomes are, at least in part, reflective of the underlying upward trend in temperatures. In the words of the director of Copernicus, “It is shocking but not surprising that we have reached this 12-month streak. While this sequence of record-breaking months will eventually be interrupted, the overall signature of climate change remains and there is no sign in sight of a change in such a trend”.

Weather attribution studies attempt to quantify how climate change and other forces are interacting to impact the probability of extreme weather events occurring. Two recent instances of extreme weather events are the heatwaves which impacted South Asia over April-May and which have continued in India into June, and the flooding seen in southern Brazil during late April into early May. According to World Weather Attribution, man-made climate change has made the South Asian heatwave 45 times more likely to occur than previously, and likely raised its intensity by 0.85 degrees C. The likelihood of the flooding seen in Southern Brazil was likely raised by a factor of more than 2 by man-made climate change, and its intensity raised by 6-9%.

## German fiscal debate remains cautious

• EU election saw a rise in far-right AfD vote

• But, centrist parties still hold large majority overall

• German fiscal policy has dropped its obsession with running budget surpluses

• But, debt brake reform proposals are modest and face political hurdles

Last week’s European election and the developments it triggered in France have raised questions about broader instabilities in the region, especially by potentially weakening the commitment to sustainable fiscal policy. In this context, Germany is often mentioned alongside France as having seen a significant shift in voting to the far-right. However, the political situation in Germany is less clear cut and recent changes do not point to a softening of its position on the need for fiscal discipline.

First, the far-right AfD won only 15 out of 96 seats and fell short of earlier polling due to a scandal. Repeated in a Federal election this implies that centrist parties would win 75% of seats under Bundestag voting rules. Second, the challenge to centrist parties from the left and right is more about social and green issues, rather than fiscal policy. Third, the fiscally conservative CDU/CSU actually gained votes, while the weakness of Finance Minister Lindner’s FDP is likely to harden its stance in an attempt to sharpen its traditional debtreducing profile. Finally, the AfD itself has some roots in fiscal conservatism and takes a hardline on EU fiscal matters.

Current debates about a supplementary budget for 2024 and a new budget for 2025 are also not signs of a shift in fiscal policy. Discussions around a supplementary budget of 0.25% of GDP are to take advantage of a larger cyclical borrowing allowed under the debt brake and could be used to ease next year’s debt brake constraint by pulling some spending forward. Even less is known about the 2025 budget discussions, but Finance Minister Lindner appears to be pushing back against departmental spending demands.

There is a realisation in Germany that the domestic debt brake has been too rigid, with earlier attempts to work round this struck down by the Federal Constitutional Court (GFCC). We review some reform proposals in this note, a prominent one having come from the Bundesbank itself. However, those reform proposals remain quite modest, and the required twothirds majority in the Bundestag to pass them is hard to see. Both the CDU/CSU and FDP are instead arguing for reductions in social spending to make way for higher government investment spending.

## Over-the-top, on purpose

The German constitutional debt brake limits the federal deficit to 0.35% of GDP in structural (i.e., cyclically-adjusted terms). The deficits of other layers of government (i.e., state, local and social security) are limited to zero. The debt brake does allow for downturns by letting some cyclical stabilisers operate and it allows the Bundestag to declare with a simple majority a state of emergency in case of large unforeseen events, such as the pandemic. However, following such emergency situations, the debt brake requires the government to pay those “exceptional” debts back over time, which means that during that repayment period the effective deficit limit is below 0.35% of GDP by the amount of the repayments.

As shown in Figure 1, the debt break has a strong deleverag ing bias. If the government runs a 0.35% structural deficit every year, growth runs in line with potential and the interest rate on government debt equals the growth of nominal GDP, then government debt will fall steadily over time. By 2050, government debt would be below 40% of GDP and would converge to almost 10% over the long term. This is because the rule does not loosen as debt falls to ever lower levels. While that seems far too strict, the architects of the rule anticipated that the economy would periodically go into recession and see shocks. The government’s Council of Economic Advisers has run simulations, which show that the effective debt path would in practice be higher, but, it would still trend down over time, moving far below the 60% limit in the EU Treaty. Of course, the exact path in the simulations depends on the type of shocks that are assumed and their frequency.

Figure 1: Germany government debt simulation % of nominal GDP, dotted line is 60% Maastricht limit
![](images/c0f3df3f8460905451cbc58000edc841e1c73fb342eccafc3d1469469a9acd25.jpg)

## Even the Bundesbank supports reforms

A couple of years ago, the Bundesbank put forward some proposals for reforming the debt brake. In terms of the main parameters, the Bundesbank proposed expanding the 0.35% deficit limit by 0.15% for net investments when debt is above 60% of GDP and to expand it by a further 0.5% for net investments if debt is below 60%. These parameters were chosen to align with the EU rules that existed at the time.

As shown in Figure 2, this still gives a downward-sloping debt profit that moves below 60% and converges to around 40% in the longer term. But, taking into account that emergencies tend to occur periodically, and that these additional debts are often repaid very slowly (e.g. over 20-25 years), the actual debt profile would possibly be closer to the 60% Maastricht limit. This is suggested by simulations from the Council of Economics Advisers, although the same caveat that this depends on the exact assumptions used still applies. In any case, even the Bundesbank’s proposal would be stricter than the new EU fiscal framework, which can allow structural deficits of up to 1.5% when debt is low enough.

Figure 2: Germany government debt simulation
![](images/ba1f58308ad62d417c5ef8b6cc1130b95a736db9e757605a43e0dd367d07aa84.jpg)

The Bundesbank’s other reform proposals were much more modest, although not unimportant. First, the Bundesbank proposed a change to the cyclical-adjustment procedure to allow more gradual adjustments of fiscal policy to revisions to economic data and to misses in tax receipt forecasts. At present, these are often taken as structural and lead to overly large and unforeseen adjustments in fiscal policy.

Second, the Bundesbank proposed allowing surpluses in the control account (i.e. balances that accrue when the deficit outperforms the brake) to be used to pay back exceptional debts from a crisis. This control account had increased to 1.2% of GDP, but it cannot currently prevent the government from having to run an even smaller deficit than the 0.35% when it has to repay exceptional debts from a past crisis.

On the other hand, the Bundesbank also suggested that the debt brake should take into account deficits that Germany is running indirectly via joint EU-level spending (e.g. NGEU). This would act to modestly tighten the brake in two ways. First, the deficit would rise by the German share of any EU debt when a new EU programme, such as NGEU, is set up. Second, to the extent that reforms link the debt brake’s deficit limit to the debt level, the latter could take into account Germany’s share of joint EU debt.

Overall, the Bundesbank proposal would allow larger deficits than currently allowed by the debt brake, but only really if

these are used to finance positive net capital spending.

Other proposals from the Council of Economic Advisers and from the Joint Economic Institutes go in the same direction. Three ideas stand out. First, shift the distinction between cyclical and structural budget items in the direction of the former to reduce an artificial pro-cyclicality of the budget. Second, to better spread the overall deficit limit across the different layers of government, given that much of the actual government investment takes place at the communal level. Third, reinstate the debt brake more gradually after a crisis. This was a particular problem after the pandemic, when the FDP rushed back to its constraints even though crisis-related spending still had to be made.

These additional reform ideas do not necessarily change the likely debt profile, but some of them would make it much easier to manage fiscal policy over the business cycle.

## The main constraint remains political

Debates about a possible reform of the debt brake have rumbled on for a long time, and the case for some changes is strong, especially after the ruling of the GFCC, but the political appetite for change remains unclear. The SPD and Greens would support changes, but the FDP would not. The FDP’s position is that social spending can be cut to make space for higher government investment, but, the ruling coalition cannot change the debt brake alone anyway and needs the CDU/ CSU to support change to have the required two-thirds majority in the Bundestag. The CDU/CSU’s position however is very similar to that of FDP: the budget is large as it is and space can be created through a better prioritisation of expenditures. It is hard to see this changing soon or easily.

What has changed is that Germany is no longer over-achieving on the debt brake requirements through the surpluses seen during the Schauble era. Nevertheless, with the deficit possibly moving below 2% of GDP this year, the direction will be towards further gradual consolidation in the next 2-3 years. For this year, that is mainly via the expiring of most remaining crisis measures from the energy crisis, but, for next year, some underlying consolidation will be necessary as well. For this, the upcoming discussion around the 2025 budget, which will be published at the start of July, will be important to watch.

Finally, what is Germany’s attitude likely to be in EU-level fiscal debates? Germany has moved to playing a more constructive role, in line with the EU’s institutional shifts seen since the sovereign debt crisis. But, given the new fiscal rules, we suspect that Germany will want to see their credibility established through consequent application.

## Brazil benefitted from the US-China trade war

Brazil’s share of Chinese imports grew for decades, with a new rise after the beginning of the trade war

China’s strong imports, commodity imports friendshoring, and diversion away from US are the main culprits

Those factors help to explain why China’s disappointing GDP growth has not impacted Brazil yet

Brazilian exports seemed to have benefitted up to 0.5% of GDP before the pandemic

China has been Brazil’s largest trading partner since 2009, driven by the need for commodity imports in the second largest economy in the World. This tend to make the Brazilian economy especially vulnerable to fluctuations in Chinese growth. However, despite the disappointing growth perfor mance in China for the best part of the last three years, the Brazilian economy has grown more than expected.

One explanation for the de-linking between China’s and Brazil’s growth is that commodity prices have been elevated since the pandemic, and Brazil’s potential GDP is higher after several reforms since 2016. A complementary explanation, which we argue more extensively in this note, is that China is switching some of its commodity imports from the US (or more broadly G10) amid a long-lasting trade war, to friendlier trade partners like ASEAN, Brazil and Russia or other major commodity exporters such as Australia (Figure 1). This seems to be a form of friendshoring on China’s part, evident in the commodity imports space.

Figure 1: China imports geographical landscape shift
![](images/04b3593105a53752885ea132bacff515b58a9a5d7e563ca709bb3d1734c3b981.jpg)

For Brazil especially, following the 2018 trade war, its share in China’s total imports has been growing, especially relative to the US performance, though there was a recovery for the US post-Phase I deal in 2020 (Figure 2). On the top of that, China appears to be importing commodities beyond what the domestic fundamentals would indicate. These factors are helping to keep demand for Brazilian products elevated, creating incentives for the local production, and making the trade surplus wider than otherwise it would have been.

![](images/80d8cc9f6ec5db6fe860b81339e0f9839e5d81833c789e82d59b42f003aac345.jpg)

## Brazil-US commodity competition

J.P. Morgan Economics has explored how the trade and geopolitical tensions are benefitting ASEAN countries and Mexico through relocation of production as the US diversifies away from China. What has been relatively overlooked is that China seems to be also diversifying away from US commodity-related exports, despite some partial reversal or stabilization in this substitution process after the 2020 Phase I deal.

This is where Brazil comes into play. The country rose to the position of leading exporter of agricultural products in the last two decades, and both Brazil and US are major producers of seven products that account for 60% of Brazil’s net exports (Table 1 and Figure 3). US and Brazil are among the largest exporters of soy, corn, poultry, beef, pork, cotton, and tobacco. Also, both countries became net exporters of oil in the last 5-10 years. These products account for around 60% of Brazil’s total exports, in a trade surplus that is now above +3.5% of GDP.

Though China is also a major commodity producer, it is the main counterpart for these commodity exports. China is a major net importer of soy, meat, corn, oil, poultry – the same products which we described a competition between US and Brazil. In other words, China is where many of the main commodity exports of Brazil and US are consumed.

Figure 3: Brazil's main commodity exports (60% of tot. exports) % of GDP. In orange: products that both Brazil and the US are top-5 producers
![](images/3ece994d48029765b2efecbbf26bc6a10d013c27c0467467630872450e821b33.jpg)

## Rotation of China imports from US to Brazil

Since Brazil and US are competitors in the global commodity market, is the US-China trade war generating China imports rotating away from the US, much like it is leading to a change in US imports away from China? In the past two decades, Brazil has grown to be one of China’s major trading partners. Its share in China’s imports increased from around 1% in early 2000s to 5% lately, with half of the gains achieved after 2018. Meanwhile, China and the US have reduced their bilateral trade reliance in the aftermath of the 2018-2019 tariff war and the US friendshoring policy since 2022. Brazil seems to have benefitted from China’s imports diversification efforts, as China purchased a large amount of commodities from Brazil in the past few years. The pace has even accelerated recently, with growth of China’s imports from Brazil significantly outpacing headline imports for most of the time since 2022 (Figure 4).

Figure 4: China imports growth
![](images/336d1de6e3cd2587c93587e7717ccbc5c94c5782e2646bdfd0ad097ca9d9127c.jpg)

If we focus on the products that Brazil is amongst the major suppliers globally (soy, crude, iron ore, sugar, corn, beef, coffee, poultry, cotton, pork and tobacco), the eleven products account for nearly 90% of China’s total imports from Brazil. Most of Brazil’s supplies to China are for soy, iron ore and crude (75% on average in the past three years), but there has been a rising purchase from China of other products recently, especially corn and beef. Brazil has emerged as the top 2 suppliers to China for almost all the eleven products, except crude (Figure 5).

![](images/e757bc69561b4ac106806953802124ca866ea5536d859511ac9429efcc37bc99.jpg)

## Two stage process

During the process, there seems to be a clear rotation of China’s imports from the US to Brazil in two stages. At stage 1, China purchased significantly higher amount of soy and crude from Brazil during 2018-2019, which can’t be fully explained by the long-term trend.

The process was somewhat disrupted after the pandemic outbreak and the Phase I agreement. On January 15, 2020, US and China signed the Phase I trade deal, and China committed to purchase an additional US\$200bn of US goods and services during 2020 and 2021, with \$32bn for agricultural products, and \$52bn for energy products. To fulfill the commitment, China significantly accelerated its purchase of US commodities in 2020 and 2021, especially corn (US\$6.3bn), soybean (\$4.0bn), pork (\$2.1bn), poultry (\$1.8bn), cotton (\$1.4bn) and beef (\$1.3bn)<sup>1</sup>. Despite the jump in purchases from the US, Brazil’s commodity supply to China remained solid, with most product lines gaining more market share (Table 2).

At stage 2, China resumed its elevated commodity imports from Brazil, and expanded the product categories to iron ore, corn, coffee, pork and beef from 2022. In the meanwhile, the US has been losing market share for most of these commodities.

Taking the two periods together, Brazil’s market share in China imports rose 23%-pts for soy, 45% for corn, and nearly 30% for beef, coffee and pork. The US’ market share was down 14% for soy, 24% for corn, 5% for coffee, 18% for pork, though up 9% for beef (Table 3). These trends happened even though after Phase I the US seems to have recouped part of the shares in corn, poultry, cotton, and tobacco (soybean post-deal gains are mostly gone by 2024).

Table 3: China imports from the US % of China global imports

We think the geographic rotation is at least partly driven by the US-China tariff war, which has made imports from Brazil more attractive. If we look at the unit import price breakdown by origin for major commodities, cost for importing from Brazil has been lower, partly due to higher tariffs on US imports (Figure 6). For example, China imposed 25% tariff on purchase of US soybeans in early 2018. The tariff rate was hiked further to 33% in late 2019. There was also 25% addition of China import tariffs on US cotton, which led to 26% tariff for in-quota US cotton and 65% tariff for out-of-quota part. Part of the retaliatory tariffs were lowered or waived in early 2020 after US and China reached the Phase I trade deal.

Figure 6: Unit import price difference
![](images/07175cac777064961861af358ca28c08e176ff547932998f1890557de9365102.jpg)

Meanwhile, China imported record-high commodities in 2023, which could not be fully explained by macro fundamentals related to underlying demand, and price effect driven by countercyclical purchase (Figure 7). We think China may have managed to build commodity stocks, though it is hard to conclude whether the purchase is to mitigate the inventory de-stocking, domestic supply-demand deficit (such as coal) or to a strategic stockpile for future use or on national security concerns. In either case, Brazil seems to have benefitted from solid commodity demand from China, especially as China manages to reduce reliance on the US.

Figure 7: China commodity imports vs. FAI %oya 3mma, both scales
![](images/1c47083b3b32e82a11200a54b22fd94e171cff914d6d57b247a936c522071d8f.jpg)

## The substitution effect

Chinese imports from Brazil and the still strong Chinese demand for commodities helped to lead a shift in Brazil’s trade balance. Looking relative to the pre-trade war trend, this new force added almost 0.5%-point of GDP in exports by 2019 and, with Chinese imports from Brazil growing strongly, it may add about 0.3%-point to Brazil’s trade balance this year if the share of Brazil exports to China return to the 2020’s highs (Figure 8). This, the improvement from supply of commodities in Brazil and the post-pandemic elevated prices help to explain Brazil’s trade balance surpassing 3.5% of GDP for the first time since 2007.

![](images/028210212cd9eda02cbfcd4991effc70cfaf8987e1e13b8442d909ce2a608b8f.jpg)

In this context, so long as the China continues diverting away some of its commodity imports from the US to Brazil or is importing commodities above the levels indicated by domestic growth rates, the effects of the slower Chinese economic growth to Brazil tend to be contained. But there’s only so much of this that can be done in the next years. Once those processes are over, Brazil would need to increasingly rely on its own domestic forces to grow in a sustainable manner if the Chinese GDP growth deceleration is more structural.

Figure 1: Sweden GDP before and after 1Q24 revision Level 100=4Q19; dotted lines before revision, solid after revision

## Sweden: No recession, but still an underperformer

After large revisions Sweden’s recent GDP path shows stagnation rather than recession

• Using NBER’s recession definition we come to a similar conclusion...

• ...although the sharp rise in the U-rate rate is an exception

• The core and interest rate sensitive parts of the economy have still underperformed DM peers...

• ...leaving Sweden in a better position to bring inflation back to target

For much of the past year the Swedish economy appeared to have the clearest recession dynamics in DM. GDP was reported to have contracted for several consecutive quarters, employment fell, the housing market deteriorated, and the LFS unemployment rate increased sharply. In turn, this contributed to a softer inflation momentum than peers, allowing the Riksbank to cut ahead of most DM central banks in May. But the revised 1Q GDP report told a different story. After making revisions all the way back to 1993 the economy had not only avoided two consecutive quarters of output contraction—the narrowest definition of a recession—but the level of GDP over the past two years could also better be characterized as a stagnation (Figure 1).

![](images/033f5f11921bb1250f98abed18aac63ba936d3bbe35ce9f368835a58022bbd23.jpg)

This raises a number of questions. Is the removal of Sweden’s recession label justified? If so, does that mean that Sweden’s interest rate sensitivity—which we back in spring 2022 argued as a key reason why Sweden should underperform the rest of DM in the coming years (see here, here, and here)— has been exaggerated? What are the implications for inflation and the Riksbank? And has the risk of a near-term recession subsided?

## Enter the two-handed recession economist

There are several ways to assess whether a country has been in a recession. One widely known approach is the one used by the US NBER Committee. This defines a recession as “a significant decline in economic activity that is spread across the economy and lasts more than a few months” where each of three criteria—depth, diffusion, and duration—needs to be met individually to some degree. Only in extreme conditions, such as the pandemic, may one criterion partially offset weaker indications from another. When assessing these criteria, the NBER specifically looks at real personal income less transfers, employment (both nonfarm payroll and household survey), real personal consumption, real wholesale-retail sales, and industrial production.

In Figure 2, the Swedish indicators closest to the US equivalents are shown. Based on these metrics we lean towards Sweden not “passing” the NBER’s recession test, although the conclusion is not clear cut. While household measures (consumption, retail trade, disposable income) have shown a fairly deep and prolonged contraction since the Rikbank began its tightening cycle, this mostly related to 2022 rather than the past 12 months. In the latter period, household measures have broadly been stagnating. Looking at the other measures—IP, employment, and GDP—these increased modestly in 2022, whereas the last 12 months has been a mixed bag. Perhaps the best argument for a recession amongst these indicators is the contraction in employment through 2H23-1Q24<sup>2</sup>. However, it is debatable whether the contraction of -0.7% during this period meets the “depth” criterion.

Figure 2: Sweden "NBER" recession indicators
![](images/8d9576f8e5bcc0078d0e76da60292ef1c3fb1760bfa211954a8c5bfbb411e27d.jpg)

Another approach is Sahm’s rule. This stipulates that a recession starts when the three-month moving average of the unemployment rate rises by at least 0.5%-pt relative to its low during the previous 12 months. By this definition (and using the LFS unemployment rate as the relevant metric), a recession began in August last year (Figure 3). In fact, Sahm’s rule indicates a deep recession. The unemployment rate has increased from 7.1% to 8.2% over the past 10 months. The move—which has been in line with typical leading indicators and our forecast a year ago—is a clear distinction from other DM countries where labor market easing has been contained.

There are, however, some caveats to the rise in the unemployment rate. First, the increase is not just related to falling employment, but also a rising participation ratio. This is atypical during recessions. Second, hours worked have recently increased, again conflicting with usual recession dynamics. Third, the registered unemployment rate, which is less volatile than the official LFS measure, does not pass Sahm’s rule as this has only increased 0.4%-pt from its low over the past year.

Overall, while a case can be made for Sweden having been in a recession, it is certainly not as clear cut as prior to the 1Q GDP report. When factoring in all indicators, we prefer to describe the economy as having stagnated.

Figure 3: Sweden labor market indicators
![](images/b7c4a679a16bd6122bba73008f5ac7ec1cc9ee69255c730e8f8597ae21bc9fcb.jpg)

## Sweden an underperformer in core GDP

With the recession label debatable the next question is whether interest rates have not had as great an impact on the Swedish economy as initially thought. In our view, there is little doubt though that the rate hikes rates have had a significant impact—and more so than in most other DM countries:

First, measures such as interest costs vs. disposable income, change in outstanding mortgage rates, and debt service ratios (both for households and nonfinancial corporations) have increased more than most other places in DM (Figures 4 and 5). Conversely, credit growth and house prices have been more depressed than elsewhere.

Figure 4: Interest payments, % of disposable income y/y, %-pt
![](images/a7609326087d4a55e4ece1e96d49ba14c8a70fd58467045e1bb18b15dc2b0348.jpg)

Figure 5: Households debt service ratios\*, 4Q23 and 4Q19 %-pt of disposable income, deviation from 1999-2021 avr.
![](images/f5e81249db385f07fef8f4204b52df7fc96c4c0e85db97a088f6ce73ad351390.jpg)

Second, since the beginning of the tightening cycle Swedish private domestic final sales—arguably a core measure of GDP—has underperformed the rest of DM, except for New Zealand<sup>3</sup>(Figure 6). Looking solely at household consumption Sweden ranks the lowest.

Figure 6: DM private domestic final sales (HH spending + investment) Level 1Q22=100
![](images/b996a40335b59a15383428ec80895eaff581b359bbc3ee170488558bc97837fb.jpg)

Third, digging into the most interest rate sensitive parts of the economy—such as consumer durables, housing, business spending on equipment, and construction—these have clearly been affected by the rate hikes. In Figure 7,

we have constructed measures of interest rate sensitive and insensitive sectors of the economy and compared them to the Euro area. The sensitive part of the Swedish economy shows a clear underperformance since the beginning of the tightening cycle, whereas insensitive parts have roughly developed in line with the Euro area.

Figure 7: Interest rate sensitive sectors - Sweden vs. Euro-area 1Q22=100; solid lines Sweden, dotted lines Euro area
![](images/1f3a49d72b6bbb341cad284bb157479c6c70df68dcea6c21be0029f5b527ddc4.jpg)

Fourth, if we go back to using GDP as the relevant measure, it is more appropriate to look at performance vs. trend. Here, Sweden also stands out among DM peers. After almost closing the gap to the pre-pandemic trend in 4Q21 (just a few months prior to the Riksbank’s first hike), the shortfall has since increased to \~6% (Figure 8). This is the widest gap in J.P. Morgan’s DM universe, except for New Zealand<sup>4</sup>.

Figure 8: Swedish GDP and deviation from trend
![](images/171274be4dbe327db6396d7cf26763f490ffb2504efbee7660b92e14fdfab848.jpg)

In sum, there is clear evidence that interest rate sensitivity has been a key reason for Sweden’s underperformance in recent years. There are, however, other explanatory factors such as a sharper fall in real disposable incomes (due to low “centralized” wages and higher inflation than elsewhere), contractionary fiscal policy, savings above pre-pandemic levels, and larger exposure to the global manufacturing cycle.

## Still a benign inflation outlook

Looking ahead, the stagnation label does not change our previous analysis that Sweden has more favorable conditions to bring inflation faster under control than European peers (see Europe’s inflation outliers charting different paths). We base this on 1) the level of GDP is lower than before the revision (Figure 1), 2) the output gap is more depressed than peers (especially for the core part of the economy which arguably is more important for services inflation), and 3) comparable measures of labor market slack point to a better demand-sup ply balance. Together, these factors have already contributed to a sharp correction in our wage-sensitive and super core measures (Figure 9). In fact, it is hard to find a single leading indicator that is not consistent with inflation at or below 2% (100 charts on Scandi inflation). In turn, this should still leave the Riksbank on track to cut rates one more time than the ECB in 2024.

In our view, the risk of sticky inflation (in relation to domestic factors) is not so much related to past economic performance now being characterized as a stagnation. Instead, it is Sweden’s interest rate sensitivity as the Riksbank has started to ease. The sensitivity thus works in both ways, meaning that a few cuts are likely to stimulate domestic demand more than DM peers (in turn lowering the risk of a near-term Swedish recession). There are already some signs of this, for example, in the housing market and consumers’ expectations of durable goods purchases in the coming 12 months. We do, however, think the sticky inflation risk is more of a story for 2025 given the number of indicators that all point to a benign outlook for the coming 3-6 months.

Figure 9: Swedish supercore and wage-sensitive inflation 3m/3m, saar
![](images/68cea0adb33954da14ab49569c51b2cb8b023ae17d545787236997dc58e022e2.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, SCB

## EM Edge: A mixed balance (of payments)

• Analysis of BoP data allows a comprehensive grasp of an economy

We resort to an “extended basic balance” approach to identify structural trends

• It shows a fair share of EM Edge economies on the positive side of the ledger...

• ...but there are still a handful of concerning stories, and others that could join them

The balance of payments is an essential indicator for EMs in general and frontier markets especially. Unfortunately, most EM Edge economies report BoP data with long lags and, in some cases, the data are of poor quality. Still, BoP analysis can reveal trends and, with reasonable assumptions, inform about a country’s outlook. We apply a standardized approach across the EM Edge space attempting to reveal relevant trends. Here we discuss the most recent data (2023) and ponder what we think is likely for the current account (CA) and financial flows this year - with implications for FX, monetary and fiscal policy, and growth.

By calculating an extended basic balance - CA + capital account + net foreign direct investment (FDI) + errors and omissions - one can identify the “structural” BoP position of an economy in relation to non-residents (Figure 1). We include errors as they are usually large and persistent in frontier economies, a reflection of informal markets likely arising from restrictions to trade or financial flows. A long-lasting negative extended basic balance is reflective of an economy dependent on volatile flows to fund its buying from abroad.

Sometimes negative basic balances are also associated with real exchange rates disconnected from fundamentals, thus further increasing the pressure on the nominal exchange rate to adjust. Such pressure can be at times avoided by leaning on more volatile FX inflows - portfolio or other investment - which could even generate, temporarily, FX appreciation. However, when those decline or even stop (due to global factors or because investors realize the building imbalances), central bank FX reserves are drained in an attempt to stabilize FX. A combination of negative extended basic balances amid low FX buffers could lead to even direr setups: besides FX weakening and FX reserves depletion, a vicious spiral of higher inflation, monetary policy tightening and/or harsh capital controls that may exacerbate the real exchange rate misalignment and also derail growth. This can create long-lasting sub-optimal equilibriums (for example parallel FX markets) that might require a deep economic recession to be reset.

![](images/47e29cd2c3b8f31c4b7167ee446488be16c67eb98aea80f2f07e9c0a5ca60719.jpg)
Source: Central Banks and J.P. Morgan \*CA + capital account + net FDI + E&O

Even countries with strong extended basic balances may come under pressure because of flows reversal. For example, over 2022 and most of 2023 EM Edge economies saw large portfolio outflows, driven by the Russian-Ukraine war and Fed tightening. These severely complicated monetary and fiscal policies, and had a negative impact on growth, particularly in Africa. Late last year brought portfolio inflows back, at least in Latam, as countries were able to access markets. In 2024 that trend has further improved, including for Africa. But elevated US yields and US dollar gains blur the outlook for market access in 2H24 and macro-stability risks remain.

That said, only six Edge economies out of 31 have particularly weak external positions: negative extended basic balances coupled with loss of FX reserves (Table 1). The worst structural external positions are present in Uzbekistan, Kenya, Nigeria, Zambia and Kazakhstan (in this order). The loss of FX reserves in Zambia has been less pronounced in 2023 and 2H23 data showed improved dynamics, but its past extended basic balance and our view that 2024 will not improve still put Zambia in a precarious position. Kenya is similar, but its extended basic balance is much worse. Because the stock of FX reserves across Africa is low (if net foreign assets or some net reserves proxy would be used, the stocks are sharply lower), there is little capacity to use FX reserves and that implies persistent pressure on FX to weaken.

% of GDP, 4q-sum, dashed columns for positive CA+Errors

The same pressure exists in Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan but the main difference is the presence of buffers - net foreign assets (NFA) or net reserves are very close to gross reserves in these two countries. Uzbekistan has an FX stock which exceeds 40% of GDP while Kazakhstan has about 15% of GDP without including the Oil Fund (about 23%of GDP), which, if included, would bring it close to Uzbekistan.

There are more EM Edge countries with a strong external position than with weak: 10 names out of 31 and five are commodity exporters (Table 2). Qatar and Kuwait need no explanation given their extremely large CA surpluses and Azerbaijan is similarly strong also due to hydrocarbons; Angola benefits from oil too while Mongolia from metals and others. Within energy-importing economies, Costa Rica and Serbia are the strongest, with large extended basic balances (4% of GDP) and similar accumulation of FX reserves (5% and 4% of GDP, respectively). Vietnam has a stronger basic balance than either Costa Rica or Serbia, but suffers from large financial outflows due to “errors ad omissions” (likely multinationals keeping un-recorded cash offshore) and accumulates less FX reserves. Finally, Uruguay and Sri Lanka have similar basic balance and reserves dynamics - though it has been the case for longer in Uruguay than in Sri Lanka, where it has just recently improved.
