A few names have not lost FX reserves in 2023 but have negative extended balances – see Figure 1. For countries like Georgia, Pakistan, DomRep, and Tunisia there is a risk to see a combination of negative extended balance and drop in FX reserves in 2024.

## Financing: not that much FDI

In financial account analysis we focus on net flows: inward foreign investment minus domestic investment abroad. Due to their stability and role in expanding productive capacity, inward FDI are the sought-after flows to cover CA deficits. FDIs can initially generate a CA deficit too, through investment and/or consumption, but by expanding a country’s supply capacity over time it is thought such imbalances will eventually correct.

Net portfolio and other inflows (of which an important part are multilaterals) could generate, temporarily, external account relief; but they tend to be more volatile, particularly the former explaining their exclusion from our definition of a country’s “structural” external position. As shown in Figure 2, financial net flows in EM Edge space are different in mag nitude, direction, and composition. Net FDI are the main source of funding for seven countries out of 25: Mongolia, Serbia, Uruguay, Vietnam, Costa Rica, Georgia and DomRep. These are mainly the countries with stronger “structural” external position identified above.

Figure 2: Edge net financial inflows (- is outflow)
![](images/ea3ad6b1c7b2c49f44f731eeb6d7365b8c978c1a41859d58017a18bf449810a8.jpg)

![](images/bd4f2f12937bf7a08ffab1b0015bf3bf01220326f64386286a4216ea6d6e53e1.jpg)

Both inward and net FDI have been fairly stable through years (Figure 3 for net FDI). Africa has been on decline and has the smallest net FDIs (around 0.5% of GDP) despite having one of the highest growth rates in the EM Edge space. GCC’s countries have inward FDIs similar to Africa but net FDI outflows because of their investment abroad looking for diversification and returns. The other three regions have levels of net FDI inflows of around 2.5% of GDP. Latam Edge has seen a decline lately, but we view this as normalization after a prolonged period of strength (from levels as high as 3% of GDP). Asia Edge is most stable and Europe Edge more volatile - due to oil-related investment but also increasing outward FDI.

A longer-term country perspective shows that net FDI flows have remained stable compared to 2015-2019 in most countries (Figure 4). Among the group with strong FDI, Mongolia and Uruguay have notably improved in 2023. In the case of Latam economies where investment has been robust, it is no surprise that this has been accompanied (most likely a product) of an improving macro backdrop. Also, Costa Rica and DomRep are attractive destinations in the face of global supply chains relocation, particularly the former, though the latter could see its supply-side reform agenda compounding nearshoring-related gains. The negative stories are Panama, Ghana and Kazakhstan, where FDI net inflows averaged only 1.5% of GDP in 2023, compared to 5.3% pre-pandemic average. Of note, Kazakhstan has started to invest abroad at the same time as less investment is going into increasing oil output.

Figure 4: Edge net FDI inflows (- is outflow) % of GDP, 4q-sum, dashed columns for positive CA+Errors
![](images/84a3796081ad8b1ca249135a40f172b049f48804bce60bc7f686d449f26a766d.jpg)
Source: Central Banks, IMF and J.P. Morgan

FDI flows into Africa Edge economies have recovered somewhat recently but remain at low levels - except for Egypt which has seen better flows in manufacturing and services benefitting from some one-offs related to the privatization effort. And the recent US\$35bn UAE land deal (US\$24bn of new money) will show a big jump in Egypt’s FDI in 2024. For the rest of Africa, domestic policies - such as law changes, administrative and capital controls - likely are the main hurdles, particularly in Nigeria. The other angle is linked to reduced investment in production of specific commodities like oil and mining sectors. Oil and mining account for the biggest chunk of exports and FX flows for Nigeria, Angola, Ghana and Zambia.

The steady decline in Chinese FDI flows into Africa - likely linked to debt issues faced by China under its Belt and Road Initiative - could also explain the limited FDI flows in the region. China is particularly significant for Kenya, Angola and Zambia. Looking forward, the further easing of global financing conditions and broad reforms should further support increased FDI flows. No big jump is expected, but rather a gradual move higher. Oil and gas sector reforms geared at attracting investments are likely to step up in Nigeria and Angola and a conclusion to the debt restructuring in Ghana and Zambia should also help FDI.

## Beware of volatility

When extended basic balances are negative, countries must rely on other financial sources (i.e. portfolio and other flows) or use FX reserves to cover the gap - if possible. Portfolio inflows have been very volatile in recent years, with all EM Edge regions (excluding GCC where there are portfolio investments abroad) alternating years of net inflwos and years of outflows. The external backdrop (i.e. interests rates, growth) in part explains the volatile dynamics of portfolio investment, but there are also idiosyncratic stories. Indeed, countries such as Ecuador, Ghana, Egypt, Georgia, Kazakhstan and Zambia, which received net portfolio inflows in the pre-pandemic average, have suffered portfolio outflows last year (Figure 5). For some, (Ecuador, Kazakhstan and Georgia) this is also a reflection of locals accumulating portfolio assets abroad.

Figure 5: Edge portfolio net inflows (- is outflow) % of GDP, 4q-sum, dashed columns for positive CA+Errors
![](images/25b9dc6ccb98b203c8ae15c16a662c75d420da4bf724af23727cd5e5852e8b09.jpg)

Finally, other inflows, of which an important part are multilateral loans and other private loans, have also proved very volatile in recent years (Figure 6). Africa is the region where other net inflows have represented a more stable financing source over the last years.

Figure 6: Edge other financial inflows by region (- is outflow)
![](images/92a215348976241d0d9e9cfab18643282fff913e69e1af31419e356be17cb730.jpg)
The authors wish to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P. Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Australia: Greater goods

• Australian goods inflation remains elevated versus global peers

This is unusual given Australia’s sensitivity to Chinese export prices is higher than the DM average

Resilient goods inflation owes to the low beta to global disinflation, CPI basket weights and non-tradable goods

These factors support our call for the RBA to be among the last DM economies to cut rates

Following rapid monetary policy tightening in 2022 and 2023, global central banks are now starting to loosen policy settings as activity cools and inflation moves lower. Rate cuts have already been delivered in Europe, Canada and Sweden, while we continue to forecast easing in the US and UK later this year. In each of these jurisdictions the inflation adjustment has been most pronounced in the goods sector, while services inflation remains comparatively high.

The Australian experience has been slightly different with inflation peaking at lower levels than many other DM economies and the RBA delivering less policy tightening compared to most other regions (Figure 1 contrasts peak/current inflation with the corresponding policy rate). While inflation has declined considerably and is approaching the central bank’s target band, above-target inflation remains quite broadly-distributed across the basket with both goods and services inflation still tracking above 3%.

Figure 1: Inflation and cash rates Inflation %oya
![](images/a9ba738417cfeb037cb2ec68949f38044e5c6119d23c88df493cb96f4769c587.jpg)

As previously noted by our global colleagues, Chinese goods deflation has been an important part of global disinflation since early 2022 and one of the main drivers behind the wedge between goods and services inflation reported in many DM economies. This makes the resilience of Australian goods prices versus global peers (Figure 2) all the more striking given well-established trade linkages, with one quarter of Australia’s imported goods basket coming from China (considerably higher than say the US).

Figure 2: Goods and services inflation Services (1Q), %oya
![](images/71bdb4d2c9dbfb36f6662d5940438ac7d09557935e20236456f464af223af462.jpg)

Our global colleagues found that between 2000 and 2022, every 1% decline in Chinese export prices lowered global core goods CPI by 7bp within six months. The specific impulse varies by region with core goods prices in the US declining by 9bp and Europe by 4bp, while the impact on Japan effectively rounds to zero. Running a similar model for Australia finds the impulse on Australian goods inflation over the same period is 9bp, while the full effect becomes evident within a year and peaks closer to 13bp (Figure 3).

For context, the impulse within a six month period is marginally higher than the DM average (6bp), but consistent with the empirical effect found in the US. We have previously explored the import price index - CPI relationship in Australia and found pass-through to domestic prices is broadly consistent with the above analysis, with underlying inflation only changing by around one-tenth of the change in import prices. Viewed through this framework it comes as little surprise that domestic goods prices have outpaced Chinese export prices , though the divergence between Australia and DM inflation is harder to explain, and warrants further investigation.

Figure 3: Australian goods price sensitive to China exports Change in goods inflation following 1%-pt increase in China's export price index, bps
![](images/9fe27c3f564a16bbb83372785220fa6ac77461ca4ec40f8f4d1799986b93460f.jpg)

## The mix matters

Aside from the limited pass-through of global disinflation to domestic prices, we attribute the strength in Australian goods prices versus peers as a combination of differences in CPI basket weights, still-elevated domestic inflation overall, and local administered pricing components for some goods.

CPI weights are derived from the household consumption survey and are intended to reflect the relative amount spent on goods and services as a proportion of total expenditure. These weights are dynamic and reviewed annually, changing in accordance with shifts in consumer spending patterns. In Australia the largest components of the goods basket include food (34%), new dwelling purchase costs (16%), furniture/ clothing (16%) and alcohol/tobacco (14%). Inflation in each of these groups remains elevated versus the DM average. Indeed, Australian food price inflation continues to run just under 4%oya (Figure 4), whereas US food inflation is tracking at about half that pace. Local tobacco prices are up 13%oya in the past year, ahead of the US (6%oya), while the wedge between alcoholic beverages is less meaningful (3%oya versus 2%oya). Both markets are experiencing deflation across household furnishings, but once again Australian prices have proven stickier with growth still outpacing the US.

Figure 4: Divergence in Australian - US inflation groups Difference in annual inflation rates, %-pts
![](images/7b08a95d7f813693692c9694a5854db3056befd1806f9e56592bb6b4e15e7d22.jpg)

To assess the influence of differing basket weights on inflation outcomes we construct a goods index where US CPI subgroup weights are applied to Australia food, clothing, tobacco, alcohol, furniture and communication inflation rates, then compare this index to the actual outcome since 2019. As shown in Figure 5, the re-weighted basket is now running almost a full percentage point below the actual goods metric, indicating price growth is strongest in sub-groups with disproportionately large basket weights relative to the US. The opposite dynamic was at play earlier in the pandemic with domestic spending patterns contributing to the lower peak in domestic inflation in Australia.

As noted earlier the CPI basket is designed to reflect the mix of spending across the household sector, meaning the reported inflation rate is the ‘correct’ measure and indicative of relative consumption patterns. Taking that as given, we also highlight the role of local taxes/inflation indexation in boosting domestic goods inflation. This dynamic is most obvious for tobacco where price changes are largely determined by structural increases in excise/custom duties and indexation, with these factors lifting tobacco prices by a combined 8% in

2024.

Figure 5: Actual goods inflation vs. reweighted index Goods basket ex new dwelling purchase costs
![](images/53b2661ade0d8785a9f3d2bf6baa6968221c56f593e29d08fc9ada583bac6bcb.jpg)

## Slow going

Australian inflation is coming down, but in a more balanced fashion than in many other DM economies with both goods and services price inflation above historical norms. Our forecast is that inflation will continue to moderate and return to the RBA’s target band, though we expect this will occur gradually with headline CPI unlikely to fall below 3% until early/ mid-2025. The RBA’s standing forecasts embeds a more drawn out return to target, but importantly don’t incorporate the Government’s electricity subsidy extensions announced in May’s Budget, shortly after the most recently released Statement on Monetary Policy.

We expect subsidy changes to be incorporated into the RBA’s next forecast round (August). While a return to the target band on annual inflation is not a requirement for the RBA to start easing policy, the expected gradual pace of disinflation alongside the resilience of the activity and labor data is consistent with our view for the cash rate to remain at 4.35% for the remainder of 2024.

## United States

• Consumer spending growth looks to be moderating in the first half

• Home sales remain weak and homebuilding is beginning to leak lower

• Jobless claims have been slowly drifting higher, suggesting modest softening in hiring conditions

Next week’s core PCE inflation figure for May expected up 0.14%, or 2.6% over a year ago

This week brought more evidence that activity growth remains modest this quarter. Most notably, retail sales in May came in a little light of expectations and prior months saw some downward revisions to estimates of sales growth. After expanding at just above a 3% annual rate in 2H23, real consumer spending looks to be growing at closer to a 2% rate in 1H24. A downshift in consumption growth was expected this year, as some of the unusual post-pandemic tailwinds like elevated excess savings started to normalize. So far, the slowing we have seen looks more like normalization rather than anything more pernicious. We expect that will continue to be the case so long as the labor market remains strong.

Regarding the labor market, lately there have been mixed signals. Most notably, the establishment survey of employment growth (e.g. nonfarm payrolls) remains super-hot, while the household survey of labor market conditions (e.g. the unemployment rate) has indicated cooling conditions. This week’s jobless claims report did little to clear up the picture. While weekly filings edged down to 238,000, that move only partially reversed the prior week’s jump, and the four-week moving average is at its highest since last September; the number of continuing claimants in the week ending June 8<sup>th</sup> (1.828mn) was the second highest since late ‘21.

The housing market continues to struggle under the weight of higher mortgage rates. For most of the past two years housing demand has been weak while housing supply remained buoyant. It’s still the case that housing demand is soft: this week saw the June homebuilders index decline and May existing home sales slipped to a 4.11 million sales rate.

But now we are also seeing more evidence that housing supply is getting hit as well. Housing starts in May slipped to a 1.277 million annual rate of groundbreakings--the lowest since mid-’20. Permits also slumped last month, and the weakness is being led by the multifamily segment.

There was some mixed news on factory activity. Manufacturing output rose 0.9% in May but that was after a few down months. Factory output was unchanged from a year earlier

and has been trending sideways over the past few years. The early June manufacturing surveys are pointing to more of the same.

Most Fedspeak this week has been stressing patience and the need to see more inflation prints like the one we saw in May to gain further confidence that inflation is on a path back to two percent. The favorable May CPI is an important input into next week’s May PCE inflation. Based on the CPI, PPI, and import prices we estimate that core PCE prices increased 0.14% in May, taking the year-ago reading from 2.8% to 2.6%. Also up next week, we expect the third print of 1Q annualized real GDP growth will be revised lower by two tenths to 1.1%.

## Consumers moving into the slow lane

May retail sales increased only 0.1%, below our and consensus expectations, and both March and April sales estimates were revised lower. The soft reading left retail sales in May flat relative to sales at the end of last year.

Key sub-aggregates also came in a little light of expectations, ex-auto retail sales slipped 0.1% and the control measure (exauto, gas, building materials, and food services) increased 0.4%, a tenth below expectations, and these measures were also revised a bit lower in March and April. Sales in the control category are up just 0.3% relative to the end of last year (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Retail sales
![](images/fda4961ef0360f9c19f86ce7aeb9d3342d34f16d8c6ee0e765852ba05c1acbc7.jpg)
Away from the control category, sales growth was also softer for building materials, which peaked in late ’22 and was down 0.8% last month, and food services, which peaked in late ’23 and was down -0.4% last month (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Retail sales
![](images/3c7531d6b222c38e3711a274a36387a6fc7afa0678c5d7f96599a64b3aba3889.jpg)

We still think real consumption may have increased 0.4% last month after slipping 0.1% the prior month. The real spending figures should look somewhat better than the nominal retail sales data thanks in part to very soft pricing in May. Steady service spending growth should also help the real spending growth number for May. We also estimate a modest downward revision to 1Q real annualized spending growth from 2.0% to 1.9%.

## Claims up

Jobless claims for the week ending June 8<sup>th</sup> jumped to 243,000, which had been the third consecutive increase and one that took the level of claims to the highest since last August. The latest print for the week ending June 15<sup>th</sup> pulled back off those highs, easing to 238,000. Meanwhile, continuing claims for the week ending June 8<sup>th</sup> were up for the seventh straight week, to 1.828 million (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Jobless claims
![](images/c47542b8eedd72d1925cade8afdc3cc5c1dfd86867c81409df57a2199fb21e3a.jpg)

## Housing is hurting

Home sales had shown a few hints of revival earlier in the year, but the most recent data point to renewed weakness. The June NAHB homebuilders survey slipped two points to 43. Existing home sales declined very modestly in May, falling 0.7% to a 4.11mm annualized pace. Broadly, sales are still hovering in a very low range given the affordability challenges at present. Inventory continues to increase from low levels, rising 18.5% over a year ago, and months’ supply is at 3.7 vs. 3.1 a year earlier, though this is still lower than pre-pandemic (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Existing homes available for sale
![](images/f7c2576ed66f5cc62d25ee099e1a1d5a692598db844e1570b2c799e00c058287.jpg)

Homebuilding also softened last month. Total housing starts in May fell 5.5% to a 1.277mm annualized pace, with singefamily starts falling 5.2% and multifamily starts declining 6.6%. That leaves total starts down at a 28% quarterly annualized pace so far in 2Q, and down 22% for single-family starts (Figure 5). Permits meanwhile fell 3.8% m/m to 1.386mm, with singe-family permits down 2.9% and multifamily permits down 5.6%. Singe-family permits have been falling since January, and are now 8% off that recent peak.

Figure 5: Housing starts and permits
![](images/58fa8f1e579e6b5c26d782ccce9fba0902d98c3891263677a0ca613f641f3a3d.jpg)

For the week ahead, attention will be focused on Friday’s release of the May core PCE inflation reading. We expect a 0.14% increase, which would lower the year-ago increase from 2.8% to 2.6%. Both nominal and real consumer spending are expected to have increased 0.4% last month. We look for orders and shipments of core capital goods to be flat to down last month, and these series have been trending sideways over the past year.

## Data releases and forecasts

Sources for all: ADP/Moody’s Analytics, BEA, BLS, Census Bureau, Conference Board, Department of Labor, Federal Reserve Board, ISM, J.P. Morgan forecasts, NAHB, NAR, NFIB, NY Fed, Philadelphia Fed, S&P Global, Standard & Poor’s, University of Michigan, US Treasury

<table><tr><td>Tue Jun 25 Sa</td><td colspan="5">Consumer confidence</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="7">10:00am</td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Conference Bd index</td><td>103.1</td><td>97.5</td><td>102.0</td><td>102.0</td></tr><tr><td>Present situation</td><td>146.8</td><td>140.6</td><td>143.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jobs plentiful</td><td>41.7</td><td>38.4</td><td>37.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jobs hard to get</td><td>12.2</td><td>15.5</td><td>13.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Labor mkt diff</td><td>29.5</td><td>22.9</td><td>24.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>74.0</td><td>68.8</td><td>74.6</td><td></td></tr></table>

We anticipate no change in the Conference Board consumer confidence index for June. Since May some salient indicators for confidence have improved: gasoline prices declined slightly, the stock market has continued to rise, and mild readings on the CPI report generated favorable headlines regarding inflation prospects. However, the Michigan consumer sentiment survey dropped again last month, its third consecutive decline.

The labor market differential (the share of workers reporting jobs are plentiful less the share reporting jobs are hard to get) will be useful to watch in light of mixed signals from other labor market indicators. In particular, while payrolls growth has remained strong, the unemployment rate has been slowly increasing, vacancies have been declining, and jobless claims have been a bit elevated, albeit possibly due to seasonal noise around the Memorial Day holiday. The labor market differential likewise has been slowly weakening, and further worsening could provide some insight into whether there could be a further rise in the unemployment rate this month.

<table><tr><td>Wed Jun 26 10:00am</td><td>New home sales</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td></tr><tr><td>Total (000s,saar)</td><td>631</td><td>665</td><td>May 634 630</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>-5.0</td><td>5.4</td><td>-4.7 -0.6</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>1.8</td><td>3.2</td><td>-8.3 -15.0</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply</td><td>8.9</td><td>8.5</td><td>9.1</td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>-3.6</td><td>0.1</td><td>3.9</td></tr></table>

We expect that new home sales declined slightly in May. New single-family home sales have been very slowly declining since last spring, consistent with the affordability challenges in the current market, although sales are nonetheless not much different from pre-pandemic levels. The stability in sales of new homes compared to pre-pandemic likely reflects in part the very limited availability of existing properties, combined with incentives that homebuilders are providing, including the option to buy down mortgage rates. Moreover, despite these incentives, homebuilder margins are doing quite well: in the set of publicly traded builders covered by our

equity analysts, gross margins are above 24% over the last four quarters, which is higher than pre-pandemic (they averaged 20-21% in 2015 to 2019). This solid profitability should continue to help builders offer incentives to get buyers into new homes in today’s rate environment.

While sales remain decent, aggressive new construction activity in earlier months means the number of units for sale has trended up, with the months’ supply of new homes for sale reaching 9.1 in April, up from a recent low of 6.9 about a year earlier. While they make up a minority of units for sale, growth in already completed homes has been the most pronounced, up 42%oya. Lower affordability combined with rising inventory is a key reason that median sale prices have remained fairly range-bound since early 2022.

We think that initial claims for jobless benefits moved down by 2,000 to 236,000 during the holiday-shortened week ending June 22. Seasonal adjustment around a relatively new federal holiday like Juneteenth presents challenges, and as such we expect some extra noise around this time. If realized, our forecast will move the 4-week moving average slightly higher to a level that is still relatively low by historical standards.

<table><tr><td>Thu Durable goods Jun 27 %m/m, sa</td><td colspan="5"></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="7">8:30am New orders</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>1.2</td><td>0.8</td><td>0.6</td><td>-0.8</td></tr><tr><td>Ex transportation</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.1 0.4</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.2 0.2 1.2</td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>1.0</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>-0.8</td><td>-0.3 0.4</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.0 0.1</td><td></td></tr></table>

We project that total durable goods orders and shipments pulled back in May after having risen the prior month. After the rapid growth coming out of the pandemic durable goods orders and shipments have largely leveled off over the past year, and this is especially the case for nondefense ex-aircraft (core) capital goods. We look for core capital goods orders to be down slightly on the month and for shipments to be flat. Business surveys for the manufacturing sector have generally been soft in May and into early June, pointing to either mild growth or contraction depending on the survey.

Outside of core capital goods, industry data point to a decline in civilian aircraft orders and shipments, which will drag down the total durable goods reading. Elsewhere in the transportation segment, the industrial production report showed a 0.6%m/m increase in motor vehicle output in May, and we expect a similar change in the durable goods report.

We expect that the BEA’s final 1Q GDP report will show that real GDP increased 1.1%q/q saar, down slightly from the 1.3% pace that was reported with the second estimate and the 1.6% pace reported initially. The small downward revision relative to the second estimate stems largely from an expected widening in the trade deficit given lower export growth, which we think was about flat last quarter. Elsewhere, changes should be minor, with a very small downward revision in consumption growth and upward revision in intellectual property investment.

We look for a further slight widening in the nominal trade balance in goods in May, based on information on volumes passing through US ports and the behavior of import and export price indexes. The goods balance had been reasonably stable from mid-2022 to the end of last year but has since widened as imports have increased while exports have remain stable.

The advance indicators report will also include wholesale and retail inventories. Inventories rose very rapidly into mid to late 2022, and total wholesale and retail ex auto inventories have since been declining slowly, though with some signs of stabilizing in the last six months. Meanwhile retail motor vehicle inventories have been steadily increasing and only recovered above pre-pandemic levels in nominal terms at the end of 2023. Industry data suggest that motor vehicle inventories continued to rise in May.

<table><tr><td rowspan="3">Thu Jun 27 10:00am</td><td colspan="5">Pending home sales sa, unless noted</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Total (mn, ar)</td><td>75.6</td><td>78.3</td><td>72.3</td><td>72.3</td></tr><tr><td>%ch m/m</td><td>1.6</td><td>3.6</td><td>-7.7</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>%oya (nsa)</td><td>-2.2</td><td>-4.4</td><td>-0.8</td><td>-4.6</td></tr></table>

Pending home sales, which lead existing home sales by one to two months, were likely unchanged in May at their post-COVID low. Existing home sales have been exceptionally weak given the current affordability challenges and the hesitancy of existing owners to give up the lower rates on their existing mortgages.

Leading indicators of pending home sales in other reports were soft last month. Mortgage purchase applications and pending home sales from Redfin both declined 3.0%m/m, while pending home sales from Zillow fell 2.4%m/m. Mortgage purchase applications have since rebounded 4.4%m/m in the first couple weeks of June, but remain at a low level. Although these housing indicators softened sequentially from April to May, they were not falling below their current cycle lows, so we do not anticipate that pending home sales will decline further from the already low April level.

<table><tr><td>Fri Personal income Jun 28 %m/m, sa, unless noted</td><td colspan="5"></td></tr><tr><td>8:30am</td><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Personal income</td><td></td><td>0.3</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.5</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Wages &amp; salaries</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.6</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Consumption</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Real consumption</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>PCE price index</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Core</td><td>0.27</td><td>0.33</td><td>0.25</td><td>0.14</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mkt-Based Core</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Core (%oya)</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.6</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mkt-Based Core (%oya)</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Saving rate</td><td></td><td>3.8</td><td>3.6</td><td>3.6</td><td>3.6</td></tr></table>

The May inflation reports that are already in hand, namely CPI, PPI, and import prices, are on the whole pointing to moderate month-over-month price growth in May after a string of firm readings in 1Q. Consequently we expect the core PCE price index rose 0.14%m/m in May, which would reduce the year-over-year pace to 2.60% from 2.75% (2.8% rounded) in April, while the headline PCE index should have risen 0.1%m/m (0.06%). In the most recent FOMC Summary of Economic Projects, the median forecast for core PCE inflation in 4Q24 was 2.8%oya, and if our forecast is realized this could be achieved with growth of roughly 0.2%m/m for the remainder of the year.

On the consumption side we look for a 0.4%m/m rebound in real spending after a weak -0.1% print in April, which would leave real spending on track to grow at least 2.0%q/q saar in 2Q, a similar pace to 1Q. The control category in the retail sales report, which excludes food services, autos, gas stations, and building supplies, rose 0.4%m/m in nominal terms, while total retail sales and food services ex autos and building supplies was flat. Unit light vehicle sales also rose 0.8%m/m, and and we expect further contributions from utilities, securities brokerage commissions, and the rest of services.

Following a soft 0.2%m/m increase in nominal disposable person income (DPI) in April we look for an acceleration to 0.4%m/m in May, stemming in part from an expected 0.6%m/ m increase in wages, which is based the payroll growth from the employment report. Given the low level of headline inflation last month real DPI would then be up a strong 0.4%m/m. With both consumption and DPI rising 0.4%m/m the saving rate would stay constant at the low level of 3.6%.

<table><tr><td>Fri Consumer sentiment Jun 28 10:00am</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td>Pre</td><td>Fin</td></tr><tr><td>Univ. of Mich. Index (nsa)</td><td>Apr 77.2</td><td>May 69.1</td><td>Jun 65.6</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Current conditions</td><td>79.0</td><td>69.6</td><td>62.5</td><td>65.0</td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>76.0</td><td>68.8</td><td>67.6</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.1</td><td></td></tr></table>

The Michigan consumer sentiment survey has been on a steady downward trend the last three months and so may decline slightly further in the final June survey. After hitting a recent high of 79.4 in March the sentiment index declined to 65.6 in the preliminary June survey, with negative contributions from both assessments of current and future conditions. The current conditions index is particularly weak, and is at its lowest level since the end of 2022.

This survey is in the midst of transitioning from a phone survey to a web survey, with a gradual phase-in that started in March and will finish by July. The University of Michigan has been running the web survey in parallel for some time, providing a comparison of how responses differ in the phone and the web survey. The web survey tends to produce more negative assessments of current conditions, and as a result the University of Michigan expects that the overall sentiment index would decline by 1.6 points per month from April to July (or 6.4 points in total) even if actual sentiment remained stable. Thus a bit more than a third of the decline in sentiment over the last three months should be attributable to this methodology update. Long-term inflation expectations also tend to be a little bit firmer in the web survey, which may have contributed to the slight increase in 5-year inflation expectations, which have risen from 2.8% in March to 3.1% in June, which is on the high end of the recent range.

Empire State survey (Jun 17)
<table><tr><td>iffusión indices, sa</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>General bus. conditions</td><td>-14.3</td><td>-15.6</td><td>-10.0 -6.0</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>-16.2</td><td>-16.5</td><td>-1.0</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>-14.4</td><td>-1.2</td><td>3.3</td></tr><tr><td>Unfilled orders</td><td>-10.1</td><td>-8.1</td><td>1.0</td></tr><tr><td>Prices paid</td><td>33.7</td><td>28.3</td><td>24.5</td></tr><tr><td>Prices received</td><td>16.9</td><td>14.1</td><td>7.1</td></tr><tr><td>Composite</td><td>46.0</td><td>46.9</td><td>49.0</td></tr></table>

The headline index for the Empire State manufacturing survey increased slightly more than expected to -6.0 in June, from -15.6 in May. New orders remained a touch below zero, at -1.0, the highest level in nine months, and shipments inched up a bit. Labor market related components remained weak in June. Notably, the index for future business conditions climbed significantly to its highest level since March 2022. Pricing related components of the index (both, prices paid and prices received) continued to moderate in June. The survey has been very volatile since the pandemic, but through the noise, the trend has been slightly negative over the recent past. Manufacturing data looked somewhat better on net lately, and this last print from the Empire State survey fits that broader pattern.

## Retail sales (Jun 18)

<table><tr><td colspan="2">om/1, 3a Feb</td><td colspan="2">Mar</td><td colspan="3">Apr</td></tr><tr><td>Total</td><td>-0.6</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.0</td><td>-0.2</td><td>04</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Ex autos</td><td>-0.9</td><td>0.6</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Ex autos and gas</td><td>-0.7</td><td>0.6</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Building materials</td><td>-0.4</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.7</td><td>-0.8</td></tr><tr><td>Control group1</td><td>1.0</td><td>0.9</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.5</td><td>05</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Ex. autos and building mat.</td><td>-0.9</td><td>0.7</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.1</td><td>04</td><td>0.0</td></tr></table>

1. Total ex. gasoline, automotive dealers, building materials, and food serv.

May retail sales increased only 0.1%, below our and consensus expectations, and both March and April sales estimates were revised lower. Key sub-aggregates also came in a little light of expectations, ex-auto retail sales slipped 0.1% and the control measure (ex-auto, gas, building materials, and food services) increased 0.4%, a tenth below expectations, and these measures were also revised a bit lower in March and April. Away from the control category, sales growth was also softer for building materials, which peaked in late ’22 and is was down 0.8% last month, and food services, which peaked in late ’23 and was down -0.4% last month.

We still think real consumption may have increased 0.4% last month after slipping 0.1% the prior month. The real spending figures should look somewhat better than the nominal retail sales data thanks in large part to very soft pricing in May (we estimate overall PCE prices barely increased 0.1% last month) . Steady service spending growth should also help the real spending growth number for May. We also estimate a modest downward revision to 1Q real annualized spending growth from 2.0% to 1.9%. Through the monthly ups and downs, it looks like the trend pace of real spending growth has slowed since the beginning of the year. In 2H23 real consumption growth came in a little above 3% annualized. So far this year that looks to have downshifted to closer to 2%. Depleted excess saving, rising debt service issues, and tempered labor market expectations may all be contributing to the moderation in the pace of spending growth.

Industrial production firmed up significantly in May, up 0.9%, handily beating expectations. Manufacturing output also increased by 0.9% in May. These firm readings came after anemic numbers for the prior two months, which were on net revised down further, both for the headline and manufacturing indexes. We were expecting a large rebound in motor vehicles and parts manufacturing, which increased by only 0.6% last month. Away from the auto sector, manufacturing excluding motor vehicles and parts rebounded 0.9% in May, after two months of declines. Mining and utilities also increased last month, by 0.3% and 1.6%, respectively.

## Housing starts (Jun 20)

<table><tr><td colspan="6">Million units, saar</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mar</td><td></td><td>Apr</td><td></td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Starts</td><td>1.29</td><td>1.30</td><td>1.36</td><td>1.35</td><td>1.33 1.28</td></tr><tr><td>Single-family starts</td><td>1.03</td><td>1.04</td><td>1.03 1.04</td><td>1.00</td><td>0.98</td></tr><tr><td>Multifamily starts</td><td>0.25</td><td>0.26</td><td>0.33 0.32</td><td>0.33</td><td>0.30</td></tr><tr><td>Permits</td><td>1.48</td><td></td><td>1.44</td><td>148</td><td>1.39</td></tr></table>

The May housing starts and permits data showed some continued moderation in both new single-family and multifamily construction activity. Multifamily starts and permits have been steadily drifting down for over a year given that units under construction are still elevated from an earlier wave of starts, and builders are attempting to manage the volume of new supply hitting the market. Single-family activity meanwhile has been softening in recent months though remains at a decent clip. While high mortgage rates are holding back demand, new single-family home sales are holding in given the ability of homebuilders to offer incentives such as mortgage rate buydowns, while competition from existing home inventory remains constrained.

Total starts in May fell 5.5%m/m to a 1.277mm annualized pace, with singe-family starts falling 5.2% and multifamily starts declining 6.6%. That leaves total starts down at a 28%q/ q annualized pace so far in 2Q, and down 22% for singlefamily starts. Permits meanwhile fell 3.8% m/m to 1.386mm, with singe-family permits down 2.9% and multifamily permits down 5.6%. Singe-family permits have been falling since January, and are now 8% off that recent peak.

Units under construction are elevated relative to pre-COVID levels for both single-family and multifamily properties, though the figures are more pronounced for multifamily construction. Multifamily units under construction are down 10% from their recent peak but are 47% above the 2019 average, while single-family units under construction are down 18% from their recent peak and are 29% above the 2019 average. Units under completion are up partly because construction times have lengthened, especially for single-family homes. Annual data shows the time from start to completion rose 11% between 2019 and 2023 for multifamily properties, and 23% for singe-family properties.

## Philadelphia Fed survey (Jun 20)

<table><tr><td>Diffusion indices, sa</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>General bus. Conditions</td><td>15.5</td><td>4.5</td><td>3.0 1.3</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>12.2</td><td>-7.9</td><td>-2.2</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>19.1</td><td>-1.2</td><td>-7.2</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>-8.9</td><td>-10.1</td><td>-6.4</td></tr><tr><td>Prices paid</td><td>23.0</td><td>18.7</td><td>22.5</td></tr><tr><td>Prices received</td><td>5.5</td><td>6.6</td><td>13.7</td></tr><tr><td>Composite</td><td>50.2</td><td>47.2</td><td>47.2</td></tr></table>

Details of the Philadelphia Fed manufacturing survey for June were mixed, with the headline index declining to 1.3 from 4.5 in May and the ISM-weighted composite holding steady at 47.2. Both readings are fairly weak and suggest continued softness in the manufacturing sector. This echoes the message from the Empire State manufacturing survey, whose headline general business conditions (-6.0 in June) has been running lower than the Philadelphia Fed survey for some months, and whose ISM-weighted composite stands at 49.1, which was the best reading since November but still somewhat low.

Looking across the components of the Philadelphia Fed survey, most of the key indexes remained in negative territory this month. New orders rose to -2.2 from -7.9 but shipments fell to -7.2 from -1.2. Shipments had briefly been looking strong in Feb-Apr but have declined in the last couple months. The employment index rose to -2.5 from -7.9, the best reading since the start of the year, and the work week rose to 4.8 from -8.3.

On the pricing front, measures of prices received and paid increased this month but are not unusually high. Prices received rose to 13.7 from 6.6, the highest reading in almost in a year. One price measure that did spike was expectations for future prices received, which jumped to 58.8 from 31.4, which is the highest reading since early 2022.

Respondents views on future business growth moderated substantially in June though they are not unusually low relative to the assessment of current conditions (future conditions are usually better than current conditions). The future business conditions index fell to 13.8 from 32.4 in May and an average of 35.1 over the prior three months. Expectations for capex spending pulled back to 12.1 from an average of 21.2 in the last three months.

## S&P Global manufacturing PMI (US flash)(Jun 21)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Jun 23</td><td>May 24</td><td>Jun 24</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Composite</td><td>46.3</td><td>51.3</td><td>52.0</td><td>51.7</td></tr><tr><td>Output</td><td>46.9</td><td>53.0</td><td></td><td>51.9</td></tr><tr><td>ISM-weighted composite</td><td>46.3</td><td>51.0</td><td></td><td>51.6</td></tr></table>

## S&P Global services PMI (US flash)(Jun 21)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Jun 23</td><td>May 24</td><td>Jun 24</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>54.4</td><td>54.8</td><td>55.0</td><td>55.1</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>52.2</td><td>49.4</td><td></td><td>51.4</td></tr></table>

The June US flash PMIs suggested that activity in the services sector continued to grow at a decent pace this month, while the manufacturing survey remained at mildly positive levels. Neither of the indices moved substantially from last month, and the all-industry output PMI was largely unchanged at 54.6, which is just a touch above where it was in May 2023 and otherwise the highest since May 2022. On balance, the surveys are consistent with our expectation that real GDP this quarter will increase at a roughly 2% pace after growth closer to 1% last quarter.

The manufacturing PMI edged up to 51.7 in June from 51.3 in May. While not an especially high reading, the message from this survey has been a bit more constructive than other manufacturing surveys recently, as the ISM-weighted composites from the Empire State and Philly Fed surveys averaged 48.1 this month, and the ISM manufacturing survey

stood at 48.7 in May. The employment index , which had been become weak late last year, continued to rebound. The output price index was mostly unchanged, and while its level is not especially elevated, there is still room to decline to get closer to pre-pandemic averages.

On the services side, the headline business activity index ticked up from 54.8 to 55.1, and the other key indices in the report were also positive. The employment index had suddenly dropped below 50 back in April, but it has recovered over the last couple months and now is back around the 1Q average. Finally, the output price index moved a little lower though is still within the range of recent months.

## Existing home sales (Jun 21)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>4.11</td></tr><tr><td>Total (mn, saar)</td><td>4.22</td><td>4.14</td><td>410</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>-3.7</td><td>-1.9</td><td>1</td><td>-0.7</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>-9.5</td><td>6.8</td><td>3.1</td><td>-1.0</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply (nsa)</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.5</td><td></td><td>3.7</td></tr><tr><td>Single-family</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.4</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>4.7</td><td>5.7</td><td>5.4</td><td>14.7</td></tr></table>

The existing home sales declined very modestly in May, falling 0.7%m/m to a 4.11mm annualized pace. Broadly, sales are still hovering in a very low range given affordability challenges and low inventory. Sales are down from a recent peak of 4.38mm in February, but remain a bit above the cycle low of 3.85mm in October. Inventory continues to increase but from low levels, rising 18.5%oya. Months’ supply is at 3.7 vs. 3.1 a year earlier, though this is still below the pre-pandemic value. With inventory squeezed the median price of a singlefamily home sold increased 5.7%oya. The price measure has been slowly accelerating and the latest y/y reading is the highest since the fall of 2022. Over the last four years the median price has now risen 48%.

# Focus: Claims could move up with falling job finding rates

When initial claims jumped last week, we were concerned that part of the recent volatility in the weekly data was due to seasonal adjustment challenges around holidays and school breaks. This week’s report was broadly in line with our expectations and the consensus, and initial claims declined by 5,000 to 238,000 (sa) during the week ending June 15. Thus, we think this partial reversal from last week’s sizeable jump is not surprising. Nevertheless, since the end of April, the 4-week moving average of this series has been trending up after moving mostly sideways early in the year. It is currently at its highest level since last September. This raises some concern about increasing layoffs and a rising unemployment rate. However, the data on actual layoffs, at least so far, does not show a rising trend in layoffs, even though some indicators of future layoffs were trending higher for a while. We think that historically tight labor markets were instrumental in keeping claims low by providing new job opportunities for newly displaced workers. A simple proxy to gauge the movements in take up rates seems consistent with this picture. Even if separations do not rise significantly, falling job finding rates for newly unemployed workers might put some modest upward pressure on claims. As we see further declines in the labor market tightness, we will keep an eye on this margin and its potential impact on the jobless claims.

Figure 1: Prospective job cuts
![](images/1dc29c33e7824c6094b3d3cf1f5a5c7172d2bf4ee5892ecff977c4eecf8f61d4.jpg)

We can sometimes get reliable advance signals about near term layoffs planned by businesses. One such measure we discussed in the past, comes from Advance Layoff Notice Data from the WARN Act. Since the WARN Act requires larger employers to notify affected workers at least 60 days before a potential mass layoff, it could potentially provide valuable information about near term worker displacement. Even though the empirical research suggests it might have predictive power for future initial claims, we have not seen any discernable increase in initial claims when this measure started to trend up mid-2022, and later stayed relatively high compared to its levels observed in late 2021[1] (Figure 1). Similarly, another gauge of future layoffs, announced job cuts measure from Challenger, Gray Christmas (CGC) has been fluctuating around a higher level than its low observed in late 2021 (Figure 1). Even though actual layoffs stayed modestly above the levels observed early in the recovery, they are down to their lowest level since December 2022 (Figure 2). <sup>5</sup>

![](images/ddc0061b519361935f1e6fba88319869c818c8751b620f8af9246dd2a5072f14.jpg)

The number of actual claimants can change with the extent of separations, but also with changing incentives to apply for benefits. For each worker, this is obviously a complicated decision. However, the likelihood of finding a new job quickly is an important part of that calculation as the process of applying for benefits could be cumbersome at times and would require steps to prove continued eligibility. A new job also likely pays more than the unemployment insurance. We can obtain a naive proxy that takes into account this margin as well, by simply multiplying the aggregate separation rate with the probability of not finding a job within the same month. This measure correlates well with the level of initial claims in the full sample (correlation 0.63 since Jan 1948), but also moved in tandem since 2021 as well (Figure 3). Unlike layoffs, this measure will also move around with changes in the job finding rate and has been moving up somewhat recently. Thus, initial claims could move up with falling job finding rates without a significant move up in layoffs.

Figure 3: Initial claims and take-up probablity
![](images/0df2522c63e98ed1a57d4a6a58bf652b6e987af3ae38591a590901e20de80d2d.jpg)

Figure 3: Euro area composite PMI DI, sa, both scales

## Euro Area

• French polls continue to point to a relative RN majority

• Euro area composite PMI suffered setback in June

• Services inflation remains sticky for now

• IG Metall asks for a 7% pay increase, which could be consistent with slowing pay growth

With one week to go until the first round of voting in the French snap legislative election, polls point to a relative majority of Le Pen’s RN. Parties have moved into three broad groups: the Renaissance party (Macron’s party, center), NFP (parties from the center-left and far-left) and RN (together with a minority of center-right ex-Les Republicains candidates). Polls currently give RN 33-35% of the votes, NFP 26%-29% and Renaissance 18%-22%. The difficulty with polls is that they need to be translated to the constituency level and with an estimate of turnout, which differs across voters of the various parties. A higher turnout could favor RN as it increases the likelihood of three candidates getting through to the second round and splitting the anti-RN vote. Despite this, sovereign yields and spreads were more stable this week.

In terms of the economic data, this week showed stickiness in services inflation, even though holiday-related effects distorted the 0.58%m/m, sa, gain in services prices upwards. While this is still a problem for the ECB, the composite PMI weakened noticeably in June, suggesting that growth slowed from a 1.5%ar to a 1.0%ar. This is below the ECB staff forecast of around a 1.5%ar and would raise the likelihood of the ECB delivering the two additional rate cuts we have in our forecast for this year (i.e. in September and December).

On the fiscal side, the European Commission recommended seven EU countries for excessive deficit procedures (EDPs), including France and Italy. These countries will need to deliver improvements in the structural primary balances of 0.5% of GDP, but the exact medium-term path will be decided in the Autumn when draft 2025 budgets are put together.

## Euro area PMI suffers a setback

The flash Euro area composite PMI fell 1.3pts in June to 50.8. That is consistent with GDP growth of 1.1%ar in June, rather than the 1.5%ar signalled in May (Figure 1). The decline was mainly due to manufacturing, which saw its output index drop 3.4pts to just 46.0, while services activity declined 0.9pt to 52.7 (Figure 2). This puts manufacturing back further into contraction, while services activity is still growing. In some sense, services are likely being supported by households’ real income gains, at least to the extent that this is lifting spending. The drivers of the renewed setback in manufacturing are

less clear.

Figure 1: Tracking Euro area GDP growth with the PM
![](images/71929309a580c254cd471bffe1d5c2b6e9155910ecdfa7d3a396e10eec0b0d7c.jpg)

Figure 2: Euro area services and manfacturing PMI output
![](images/8a4689973aaf503ad01bc90bc9137d35af0fb3bfdc01e8ea9a55dc5f8e1cf882.jpg)

One possibility is that the uncertainty created by the EU election, at least in France, has driven the PMI lower. The future output index certainly declined and the French election was mentioned in the press release for the French PMI (Figure 3). But, while the French PMI was already lower than in other countries, it declined by less in June than others. Hence, the risks is that the PMI reflects a broader softening, even if it is not entirely clear what may be driving this. On a more positive note, Euro area consumer confidence improved further in June, although the country details are not yet known.

![](images/f3eeb34bffb1de0b52919adeafa4d988363f3939ead22dbdb77fa755e191eaa1.jpg)

## Services inflation still sticky

This week’s final HICP report for May left core inflation unrevised: it rose 0.2%-pt to 2.9%oya, which implied a 0.36%m/m, sa, increase in core prices. But, the balance between services and core goods inflation was shifted towards the former. Hence, services prices rose 0.58%m/m, sa, in May, while core goods prices fell 0.04%m/m, sa. In the flash report, these were reported as increases of 0.53%m/m and 0.04%m/m, respectively. As a result, the higher frequency momentum in services inflation held above a 5%ar (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Euro area services inflation %oya and %3m saar
![](images/91405373fd2ce569e1cbbb9523291073f61c92138973fd7cad87e9fa2e76198a.jpg)

The new details suggested that perhaps 0.2%-pt of the increase in services prices in May was due to holiday-related items, which points to a final leg of the Easter distortions. But, even controlling for this and for earlier effects from the higher German VAT on restaurant meals and the very large increases seen in insurance prices, services inflation looks solid and sticky. Hence, for the ECB, it will be important to see some reassurance about the momentum in the run-up to its September meeting. We expect this to materialize and therefore we stick with our call for a next rate cut in September. But, near-term risks to inflation remain to the upside, even if the ECB has been getting some relief from weakness in core goods inflation.

## IG Metall asks for 7% pay increase

This week, the IG Metall union made a 7% pay demand for the upcoming talks in the German manufacturing sector. This would affect almost 4 million workers, with talks likely taking place mostly in November and December. In deciding on the pay demand, IG Metall acknowledged that some firms were facing challenges, but it noted the high orders backlogs, the improved growth outlook and that workers still faced a high price level, while the inflation compensation payments were one-offs that provided only temporary help.

As shown by Figure 5, IG Metall typically gets around half of what it asks for, with employers having formulated an offer from their side of a pay freeze. The outcome will be impor-

tant. Many deals currently being struck in Germany in other sectors are still responding for the first time to the high inflation of the past two years, whereas the last IG Metall deal already included the full tax-free inflation compensation payment of €3000. Our forecast assumes that the sharp pickup in Euro area wage growth is mainly a response to high inflation, rather than to the tightness of the labor market, and that wage growth will slow now that headline inflation has declined substantially from its peak. Looked at in this way, the 7% demand would be in line with our forecast if the result is around half of this. It could be a bit firmer, but it is noteworthy that some recent demands in other sectors have still been in double-digits. Hence, it is quite moderate relative to other demands.

Figure 5: Wage negotiations in the German metal/engineering sector
%oya, in years without blue bars, the previous year's deal applies
![](images/2cbfffca8a8e8b322a4b8ed6ab1ffce8b6a17759d2328df9df36b71fff48bbd7.jpg)
Source: IG Metall, J.P. Morgan

## EU proposed EDPs for five countries

This week, the EU recommended deficit-based EDPs for five Euro area countries: France, Italy, Belgium, Slovakia and Malta. Four other Euro area countries were investigated (Spain, Finland, Slovenia and Estonia), but they were not proposed for EDPs. This is because the breaches were judged to be temporary or due to exceptional factors.

In Italy, the budget deficit has been very high in part due to the superbonus scheme. Eligibility criteria have now been tightened and therefore the deficit is likely to fall this year from around 7.5% to around 4.5%. But, this leaves the EC forecast showing no further improvement in the structural primary position next year. For France, the EC forecast does how an improvement of the structural primary position of 0.5%, which is the required adjustment under a deficit-based EDP, but this leaves no room for an RN-led government to implement any of its fiscal plans.

The exact adjustment paths will also depend on how the new fiscal rules are implemented later this year as the paths will depend for most countries on agreed net expenditure paths. For now, we continue to assume that fiscal policy tightens by around 0.5% of GDP next year, but the latest EC forecast has a tightening of only 0.1%. The latter is however based on a no-policy change assumption and is incompatible with the requirements of the new fiscal rules, not just in EDP countries.

strength in recent negotiated pay data, reflecting the timing of pay deals. From this perspective, German retail sales are likely to perform better going forward than the recent trend. A number of countries will release preliminary inflation estimates before the Euro area release on July 2. Euro area core inflation increased a tenth in May due to a rise in services price inflation (partly resulting from a base effect in transport prices) and services prices were sticky on the month (even after controlling for the Early easter effect). We expect the

price pressure in services to fade over time. Price indices already suggest that strong margins are not contributing as much to rising prices and the fading of wage inflation over time should reduce cost pressures for corporates.

Bank lending has remained weak in recent months, but that implies stabilization after a large downshift seen a year ago. Bank lending rates have edged lower and economic growth is picking up, which could lift bank lending. However, both households and nonfinancial corporates are running large saving positions and therefore the need for external finance could be modest. As a result, only a gradual improvement is likely.

Output and surveys

<table><tr><td colspan="6">Construction output Apr</td></tr><tr><td colspan="6">Feb Mar</td></tr><tr><td colspan="6">Euro area</td></tr><tr><td colspan="6">%m/m, sa 04 0.1 0.1 -0.5</td></tr><tr><td colspan="6">%oya, sa 14 -1.7 00 -0.9 -0.9</td></tr><tr><td colspan="6"></td></tr><tr><td colspan="6">Consumer confidence (prelim)</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td></td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3">Euro area (European Commission survey)</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>% balance of responses</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Consumer confidence</td><td>-14.7</td><td>-14.3</td><td></td><td>13.8 -14.0</td></tr></table>

Euro area flash June consumer confidence increased 0.3%-pt to -14.0%bal, continuing the upward trend it started last autumn. In terms of level, confidence remains 0.7 standard deviations below the 1999-2019 average. No details were published, but it is likely that improving real incomes are lifting sentiment. And, as the constellation of still strong wage growth and lower headline inflation is set to continue, confidence could rise further in the next few months. No country details are available either and an interesting question is the potential impact of the lower house election in France, with some decent likelihood that Le Pen’s party could come to power (in a cohabitation). The 2017 and 2022 presidential election campaigns did not seem to have had an impact on confidence back then, but the likelihood of Le Pen getting to power was low.

## Japan

• Ueda leaves door open for July rate hike

• Activity and inflation reports mixed through 2Q...

… but incoming data unlikely to materially sway BoJ’s immediate policy path

• Next week: Retail sales up, IP rebounds in May

The past week’s data flow was punctuated by Ueda’s remarks on the possibility of a rate hike in July, largely repeating comments he made at the prior week’s monetary policy meeting. By again stating that a hike is possible, we think Ueda aims to leave all options on the table next month, particularly in the face of a renewed yen slide.

Against this backdrop, market views for the July meeting appear to be converging around two scenarios. The first is for a relatively small shift in balance sheet policy that would be coupled with a 15bp rate hike. In other words, a minor reduction in near-term bond purchases would be announced at the same time as a hike to 25bp, albeit the BoJ could also signal a later, more significant, reduction in purchases over a 1-2 year horizon. The second scenario is for an immediate material reduction in JGB buying; i.e., a near-term inflection in liquidity conditions via QT, but no policy rate hike in July.

Figure 1: Japan Change in govt. debt stock by holder
JPY tn, annualized m'thly chg. in BoJ JGB stock, less chg. in total JGB stock, 6mma
![](images/99a9cc2830d75401c5ad9d6576d557da5170cca244369ee26caa04e3d6b199aa.jpg)

We noted last week that a “substantial” cut in JGB purchases announced in July could raise the hurdle for a rate hike at the same meeting. While acknowledging the risk that a July policy hike gets pushed back, we maintain our baseline forecast for a 15bp hike next meeting at this point. Taking a step back, either scenario above would represent an important move toward a reduction in policy accommodation. Indeed, the BoJ has already reduced its footprint in the JGB market relative to its large-scale net bond buying in the peak days of QQE and during its aggressive defense of the 10Y YCC ceiling more recently (Figure 1). We suspect that the BoJ’s intent is to desensitize the market somewhat to what will be an important policy meeting in July.

Between now and the July meeting, the BoJ’s 2Q Tankan survey, released July 1, will be closely watched, as will incoming inflation and consumption data. But we still think it would be a mistake to put too much weight on the high-frequency data flow in judging the BoJ’s policy path. We remain of the view that the BoJ is on something of a pre-determined course that will not be materially swayed by the economy’s short-term cyclical stance. By first removing YCC and negative rates, the BoJ has set the stage for a “substantial” reduction in bond purchases and Ueda has signaled that hikes above the zero bound are coming. If we are correct, then the BoJ’s dragging its feet in June was less of a reaction to incoming data weakness, and more an attempt to lay the groundwork for the next important shift in policy that is now in the cards.

It is in this context that the mixed bag of incoming data should be interpreted. Activity indicators through 2Q are painting a varied picture of demand, with a surprise fall in the services PMI implying activity stalled this month. We look for monthly rises in next week’s May retail sales and production reports, but the risk is that a sustained auto sector drag— owing to temporary output suspension amid an ongoing safety testing scandal—weighs on output, exports and sentiment into early 3Q. (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Japan activity data and survey summary %m/m sa; pts. chg in index. IP and retail sales are JPM May f'cast
![](images/64345aba0a2c8b151338a4e8271db0ca09c8aa4d484276ea0db8b3153a6593ee.jpg)

## Underlying inflation muted in May

The May nationwide official core (ex. fresh food) CPI inflation jumped, as expected, while underlying inflation remained rather muted reflecting weak domestic demand. The May official core (ex. fresh food) inflation jumped 0.5%m/m, sa, pushing up oya inflation to 2.5% from 2.2% in April (Figure 3). The May jump was attributable to a rise in the renewable energy charge, that had been temporarily reduced for a year until April as a part of the government measures to curb energy inflation. With the removal of the temporary measure, energy inflation rose 2.7%m/m, sa, in May. We expect further rises in energy inflation in June and July, as the government will remove electricity and gas subsidies over the two months.

Figure 3: Core CPI inflation measures
![](images/e72f60dd24949574b72d334b8172a7b59d4cb5f269fa7e8ebfc807ce2f9aead6.jpg)

However, the global standard measure of core inflation (ex. food and energy) remained rather muted, with a second consecutive monthly decline in services prices, down 0.1%m/m, sa. Reflecting receding cost pressures over the past months, services inflation has moderated in recent months, particularly for administered prices such as public transportation (1.5%oya), education (-1.1%) , and medical care (1.1%). Other services price inflation remained elevated, and core goods prices rose 0.2%m/m,sa, to 2.1%oya. While core goods inflation had trended down since the middle of last year, we now start seeing some signs of a reversal.

We continue to think the moderation in underlying inflation will be transitory. Energy inflation likely will continue to rise sharply for the next two months, which likely will feed into underlying inflation with a lag. We expect this dynamic to give the BoJ confidence in achieving its 2% inflation target.

## June PMI disappoints on services drop...

Japan’s all-industry flash PMI fell 2.5 points to 50.0 in June, reflecting a unexpectedly large 4-pt plunge in the services PMI (Figure 4). The drop left the services index at 49.8, its first dip below the expansionary 50-threshold since mid-2022. By contrast, the manufacturing index slipped marginally by 0.2 points to 50.1, largely as we had forecast, and underlying details were relatively upbeat. The output index moved above 50 for the first time in 13 months, consistent with our forecast for a recovery in production momentum this quarter. That said, we suspect the drag from auto plant shutdowns will become more apparent in early 3Q.

While demand for services slowed, commentary released alongside the PMI data also highlighted labor constraints that are limiting services providers’ ability to operate at full capacity—even as they attempt to hire additional staff. We think this dynamic will lead to sustained pressure for higher wage growth, eventually supporting consumption. But at this juncture, the June PMI is pointing to a stall in activity in late 2Q.

Figure 4: Japan Headline PM
![](images/e1e567511cc041d204da327980c48344d2d348c4cfc45e6ca478132ffd7a41b5.jpg)

In the details, services activity fell 3.9 points, its largest decline since the pandemic, with a 3-pt fall in the new business index and a 4.1-pt drop in new orders from overseas. There was a 1.3-pt drop in the employment index, its sharpest fall in 9 months, likely reflecting difficulties in securing workers. The internals of the manufacturing PMI were more upbeat. While the headline slipped 0.2 points, there was a large 1.9-pt rise in the future output index, albeit new orders (-0.8-pt) and new export orders (-0.4-pt) softened.

## … adding to mixed 2Q demand signals

The data through 2Q are painting a mixed picture of demand. Capex appears to be recovering after stalling in 1Q, but a sustained auto sector drag is weighing on exports and sentiment. Following a slip in small business sentiment through May, large manufacturers reported a decline in confidence through June, and exports slipped in mid 2Q. In the details:

April core machinery orders declined by 2.9%m/m sa, broadly in line with our expectations. This decrease follows significant gains over the previous two months, resulting in a run rate of 36.9%3m/3m saar, suggesting some upside risk to our forecast for a 3%q/q saar gain in capex this quarter (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Japan GDP-based capex and core pvt. machinery orders
![](images/29c96be1ca636a94c07ffcba78fb6d8c0e1d49e2cca4e5f18fdeeb19696e1906.jpg)

May export volumes fell 1.4%m/m sa, weaker than our expectation for a moderate rise, largely reflecting a drop in auto shipments. The fall last month left overall exports down 2.1%3m/3m saar, and while the pace of contraction has moderated, the trade data are coming in weaker than our forecast for a 10%q/q saar rise in GDP exports this quarter. Import momentum is running stronger, and the data are suggesting a modestly negative contribution to 2Q GDP. We will revisit our forecast with the June data (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Japan Real exports and imports %3m/3m saar
![](images/0020d0ccb51b7fd8c35a7267d0cfe0a3d4ecaa8395acd19b7354ed6d4b66d1a7.jpg)

Figure 7: Japan Real exports by destination 2015=100, deflated and sa by J.P.Morgan
![](images/e8326a8f33f6a43bc2bbc2930db0a67f1713fea878f07ad08ef879a432710a4c.jpg)

While our baseline remains for external demand to recover moderately through midyear, the risk is rising that further halts in auto production and shipments weigh on export growth. May auto export volumes stalled after a rise in April, falling 0.4%m/m sa and were down 7.5%3m/3m saar. The drop in auto shipments to the EU has been particularly sharp: volumes fell 23.5%m/m sa and in level terms are at their lowest since the pandemic (Figure 7). Tech export volumes were stronger, up 2.6%m/m sa, albeit not quite reversing a 2.8% fall in April.

The June Reuters Tankan survey suggested a deterioration in sentiment among large manufacturers, with the index falling 3 points to a level of 6. The decline was primarily driven by negative sentiment in the steel/nonferrous metals sector and the auto/transport equipment sector. By contrast, sentiment among non-manufacturers improved 5 points to 31, nearly reaching its highest level in the current cycle. We continue to note a sharp rise in sentiment in the construction/real estate sector, which rose almost 20 points to its highest since December 2019, potentially on sustained rises in property prices (Figure 8).

Figure 8: Reuters Tankan, construction and real estate DI, level of conditions
![](images/0d435018ad927848b7f313fefcf6b6975abf46b50678673e7d8106467936746b.jpg)

## Machinery orders (Jun 17)

## Canada

Bank of Canada felt confident in June that progress toward the inflation target would continue

We forecast headline and core inflation eased to 2.6%oya in May

Existing home sales declined in May; housing starts rose to a seven-month high

Next week’s May CPI report will continue to shape Bank of Canada views on evolving inflation dynamics. In the minutes of the June monetary policy decision, the Bank of Canada emphasized that it would continue to closely monitor the evolution of core inflation. Notably during the deliberation, the Governing Council considered the merits of waiting for additional monthly CPI data but in the end there was consensus that there had been sufficient progress to warrant a first policy rate cut. The members of the Governing Council agreed that the recent evolution of core inflation—with both of the Bank’s preferred core measures easing for four consecutive months and within the Bank’s target range—had increased their confidence that progress toward the 2.0% target would continue. The Bank allowed that “if inflation continued to ease and remained on a sustainable track to the 2.0% target, it was reasonable to expect further cuts to the policy interest rate.” We forecast that both headline and core CPI eased a tick to 2.6%oya in May, similar to the pattern in four out of the last five reports——since December with the exception of January, the annual pace of headline CPI has matched the annual pace of core CPI (excluding food and energy) (Figure 1). And both have eased from 3.4%oya in December to 2.7%oya in April. We expect the Bank of Canada to cut the policy rate again in 4Q24 with the forecast heavily dependent on continued easing in underlying inflation toward the 2.0% target through the summer and into the fall.

Figure 1: CPI
![](images/d9edb2bc275b814005487b5f129a736282c3ee2940fc0a33c442992129201e13.jpg)
Source: Statistics Canada, J.P. Morgan

We expect weakness across the energy categories in the May CPI, with prices at the pump lower on average during the month of May compared with April along with fuel oil prices. If we are right, the gasoline CPI will be down (nsa) for the

first time since January. We estimate further drags from slower growth in monthly rental costs and mortgage interest costs in May; we expect that easing pattern to continue in the months ahead. At the June meeting the Bank of Canada noted the risks surrounding shelter inflation in an environment of easing monetary policy. Cuts to interest rates could lead to renewed overheating in the housing market, given the pent-up demand.

## Housing activity stabilizing at lower levels

The latest data on Canada’s housing market show activity stabilizing below the elevated levels seen in the years immediately following the pandemic (Figure 2). Existing home sales receded 0.6% in May, marking a fourth consecutive monthly decline in the series published by the Canadian Real Estate Association. The six-month trend in existing home sales has largely moved sideways this year right around the level seen in the run-up to the pandemic. Higher mortgage rates are likely acting as a drag on the sector, although with the Bank of Canada kicking off its easing cycle earlier this month interest rates have moved somewhat lower recently. While the expectation is that the central bank will proceed with further cuts as inflation continues to come down, our view remains that the Bank will lean toward a cautious and gradual approach to easing.

Figure 2: Housing market
6-month moving average, thousand units, saar; both scales
![](images/ff3f8c057fbfb1d46dcb69d3bfbf5eac8853bfc488de919b69547ba923a7b0a2.jpg)

Housing supply has been struggling to keep up with housing demand, which has in turn kept upward pressure on home prices and rents. This week’s data from Statistics Canada showed new house prices firming 0.2% last month on the heels of a very similar April rise. That said, recent strength in the housing starts data is an encouraging sign for housing supply. The annualized pace of housing starts rose to a sevenmonth high of 264,506 units in May according to data from the Canada Mortgage and Housing Corporation. Housing starts have now advanced a total of 6.6% year-to date, although largely on a move higher in the multifamily category (which tends to be more volatile) while single starts have basically moved sideways over the same period. CREA data also showed a 0.5% rise in new residential listings last month.
