## Mexico

• We expect Banxico to keep rates on hold next week

• CPI to show road ahead remains treacherous

• Apparent positive underlying GDP trend blurred in April data

• May trade data could bring some clarity (solid exports, imports up)

Next week, we expect Banxico to keep rates on hold at 11% . We defined our change in call as a “head scratcher” for the board considering the incoming policy and institutional challenges on the one hand and the continuing disinflation gains on the other.

Precisely, the guidance and actions from Banxico will likely be directed at communicating its understanding of current events and risks, acknowledging the differences between “permanent” and “transitory” shocks, which will determine forthcoming actions. To this point, the fact that the FX shock is so recent and complex and that events are so fluid, we believe that the board will implicitly call for caution—particularly considering the risk of a material pass-through to inflation and, specifically, to core (goods) inflation.

We expect a unanimous decision in which the board does emphasize caution and prudence but also calls for flexibility considering that inflation has delivered encouraging news in the last months. We would not be surprised if the board marginally cuts its year-end forecasts slightly below 4%, but with the currency on a weaker path, a more reasonable action would be to keep forecasts unchanged this time. Furthermore, the encouraging signal in the latest May CPI print, particularly in terms of services, has yet to be reinforced.

In fact, our preliminary forecast for June CPI (we will get data for 1H Jun next week, and we expect 0.16%2w/2w and 0.22%2w/2w gains in headline and core CPI, respectively) suggests core CPI likely regained some momentum this month, firming 0.32%m/m, sa. What’s more, the modest pickup would come on the back of a 0.44% gain in services (0.54% increase in services ex. housing and education), which the central bank has been monitoring particularly closely (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Core CPI - Other Services
![](images/8481d3a4ef43bc44ffc98c6cf0b42299bcb5c6f43bc02d3fad3c0ca911a65ba4.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan. Dashed line is 5-year average. Forecast is labeled

We eye 25bps cuts in September and December, bringing the policy rate to 10.50% (Figure 2). For next year, weaker growth and a tight fiscal stance are consistent with rates around 9% by year-end and the currency at 18.50. Still, from a country-risk perspective the outlook remains cloudy, which could bring credit rating agencies back to the fore and push Banxico to stop at a higher than expected 9%. But it is too early to tell.

Figure 2: Banxico policy rate outlook
![](images/f8e123e9847665f541c8aab572251e8f2b907ed822d357b781fc168f20f74ee9.jpg)
Source: Banxico and J.P. Morgan forecasts. Outlook updated June 7. June 24, Dec 24 and Dec 25 forecast are labeled

## Blurry underlying activity momentum

April data releases also signaled very robust private consumption early this quarter, with retail sales up 0.5%m/m, vs. our -0.3% and service-sector income surging 1%. This news, again, was countered later in the week, with the GDP proxy tumbling 0.6%m/m on an unexpected drop in services output; services income and output, though not tracking each other tick by tick, seldom move in opposite directions. We think the underlying trend in domestic demand is solid (though not as much as in 1Q), but data between between Feb-Apr have been subject to unusual seasonal noise that makes it complicated to assert this with high conviction. In fact, speaking to GDP in a vacuum, it is tracking well below our current-quarter forecast, with the April level consistent with a 1% annualized contraction in 2Q (Figure 3).

Figure 3: GDP proxy %m/m, sa

![](images/0f11955c3011a19fa5e65d128a84a9257c3cb98aa26d63c330f86cdb1520b334.jpg)
Source: INEGI, and J.P. Morgan forecasts
Source: INEGI

We will watch May data closely, as they should be clear from seasonal distortions to some extent. Next week’s May trade data will be the first to come. We look for manufacturing exports to have leapt, consistent with strong auto exports, survey-based external demand, and US manufacturing. Meanwhile consumer and capital imports likely remained on an uptrend (even if not as steep as in prior months), boosted by domestic demand and a strong currency.

## Brazil

• COPOM interrupted the easing cycle with the policy rate at 10.50%

Unanimous decision should contribute to reduce future policy uncertainty

• But lack of announcements on fiscal reinforces concerns

• Focus turns to BCB minutes and Inflation Report, as well as economic indicators over next week

A number of forces have conspired to both sour the mood around Brazilian assets and raise the all-important inflation expectations. These include the change of the 2025 fiscal target, persistent pressures on recurrent expenditures, reduced scope for increasing revenues, the split May COPOM decision, and tighter global financial conditions. We have argued that this process could be stopped through two complementary lines of action. One, was the BCB unanimously keeping the policy rate unchanged this week to reinforce the commitment with achieving the midpoint of the inflation target. Two, is government announcements that strengthen the notion of commitment to a fiscal adjustment with spending measures.

Over the past week, the first line of action was delivered by the COPOM. BCB unanimously kept rates on hold at 10.50%, with a statement that highlighted the need for caution and vigilance amid rising BCB inflation projections and inflation expectations, resilient economic growth, and uncertain global outlook. In addition, BCB’s projections disavowed the market pricing of hikes by showing in its model that a stable SELIC rate until the end of next year would be sufficient to bring inflation at 3.1%, very close to the midpoint of the target (Figure 1). Accordingly, the COPOM indicated it will keep the policy rate restrictive for a sufficient time at a level that consolidates the disinflation process, as we anticipated in our preview.

Figure 1: SELIC rate path for 2024-2025
![](images/21d4f16155ff68bea9821dc6c641d4d2edb50496c27eaa9fa4e2bde70e81e88a.jpg)
Source: BCB, Bloomberg Finance L.P., and J.P.Morgan forecasts

This communication looks consistent with our call of a stable SELIC rate at 10.50% for the foreseeable future, though we reaffirm the lack of visibility especially beyond the next couple of meetings. While global conditions will be a factor in the next decisions, the direction and magnitude of future policy changes appear tightly linked to how much credibility Brazil’s economic policy can regain.

Outside of monetary policy, there was little progress on new measures that could reinforce the government’s fiscal targets and its fiscal framework. On Tuesday, President Lula reckoned that the economic team should prove that there is an actual need to cut expenditures (‘Leia a transcrição da entrevista de Lula à “CBN”,' Poder360, June 18). However, Lula’s focus on tax benefits that surpass 5% of GDP is a sign that the bar for stringent measures on spending is high, with little urgency to present them. The president also renewed criticism of high interest rates.

Until the next COPOM meeting, set for July 31<sup>st</sup>, there will be plenty to monitor. In the short term, focus will be on inflation, labor market, external and fiscal data, on the top of additional communication from the central bank in next Tuesday’s meeting minutes and Thursday’s 2Q Inflation Report. Over the coming weeks, we expect continued scrutiny on potential fiscal measures, with actual announcements more likely closer to the third fiscal review (July 22). Marginal data suggest that the government will have to enact stronger budget cuts than in May’s second fiscal review, as the tax collection growth fails to live up to official forecasts (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Federal revenue and activity index
![](images/fa091865e91c0efc68ec1b7dd5d79335917c594f05adf0e589f68d8dcca6d8a6.jpg)
Source: RFB, BCB and J.P. Morgan

A large part of the fiscal disappointment is related to non-recurrent revenue, such as those linked to tax litigation settlements, but part may reflect the negative shock associated with Rio Grande do Sul’s floods. The impacts of this shock are not limited to a fall in the tax burden, with the public calamity decree that is valid until the end of 2024 paving way for transfers and direct expenses. With more clarity on the extent of the damage, the government increased by over 50% the resources destined to a BRL5.1k single-payment to displaced individuals. Even so, our tracking suggests that those direct transfers represent the smallest share of the expected support for the region this year (Table 1).

## Argentina

The Treasury reported a strong primary surplus in May for US\$2.6bn equivalent, the fifth in a row

Headline balance (inc. interest payments) posted a 0.4% of GDP surplus YTD, also the largest since 2008

• Primary revenues saw a strong improvement on the month, favored by income tax collection

• We revise higher our 2024 annual primary surplus forecast to 0.5% of GDP

Risks remain tilted to the upside, as the Omnibus bill 2.0 and fiscal package return to the Lower House for final approval

The Treasury reported a strong primary surplus in May for US\$2.6bn equivalent, the fifth in a row. The fiscal result confirmed what was anticipated by the May tax collection report (see note). The YTD primary surplus reached 1.1% of GDP, as compared to the -1.1% of GDP shortfall reported in the same period of 2023, representing the largest recorded in real terms on the series records since 2008.

The headline balance (including interest payments) posted a 0.4% of GDP surplus YTD, also the largest since 2008 (Figure 1). This represents a sizeable fiscal consolidation when compared to a -2.1% of GDP shortfall in same period of 2023. On a last 12-month basis, headline balance is running at a -0.9% of GDP deficit, as compared to a 4.4% shortfall by year-end.

![](images/14ed410da940a78c29e490dcbb1e7ed5b7d4fe2912b614e58bc782e1b7f2f872.jpg)

The main news last month was the strong improvement in primary revenues, with sequential momentum landing in positive territory after 8 months in the red. Indeed, primary revenues increased by 9.8%3m/3m, saar in May, coming from - 19.5% the prior month. On an over-year-ago basis, primary revenues are up by 5.9%oya on the month (-2.5%oya YTD), as compared to -7.1%oya average in the prior three months.

As anticipated in our recent note, the strong reversal in primary revenues on the month has been mainly explained by extraordinary income tax collection resulting from end-23 FX devaluation (+92.8%oya in real terms or US\$2.1bn). In addition, (temporary) “PAIS” tax saw another 253.5%oya (+US\$0.6bn) jump in May, favored by Bopreal issuance, while foreign-trade related taxes also saw a rebound on the month (+21.9%oya or +US\$0.8bn). When controlling for one-off increases in income and “PAIS” taxes, on a sequential basis, primary revenues were down by -1%m/m, sa on the month, with negative momentum accelerating to -27.7%3m/3m, saar.

Figure 2: Primary revenues and expenditures %oya last 12 months, deflated by CPI
![](images/72492990544a2395396eea8a5ad3b3c9094613ea9b387e9b576eca40b75abdc2.jpg)

On the spending side, the over-year-ago contraction in primary spending remains broad-based, with overall spending down by 28.6%oya in real terms in May, as compared to -29.5%oya in the prior three months. On a sequential basis, primary spending also declined by 7.6%m/m, sa, interrupting 3 months of sequential increases. As emphasized previously, the primary spending decline has been spearheaded by social transfers, explaining 41% of the YTD spending adjustment, followed by capital spending (23%), consumption and provincial transfers (18%) and economic subsidies (13%, Figure 3). In terms of levels, primary spending is 27.2% below the levels seen in July-November 2023, with social transfers down by 20.9% and capital spending by a remarkable 79%.

Figure 3: Factors explaining YTD expenditure decline % of total spending contraction
![](images/55a1c852320a002f02f0ce7e2b7761034b3ecbb5f8a44a25cf1560806be4e5e4.jpg)

## 2024 primary surplus forecast revised up

Following the stronger-than-expected May fiscal result, we revise higher our 2024 annual primary surplus forecast to 0.5% of GDP. In addition, we continue to flag upside risks to the forecast, as the Omnibus bill 2.0 and fiscal package return to the Lower House for final approval. Attention will be placed on whether the ruling party will be able to overturn some of the modifications made in the Senate, particularly those related to the rejection to the re-incorporation of the income tax for the 4<sup>th</sup> income category and the changes on the personal goods tax. Note both changes accounted for around 0.5% of GDP in additional fiscal revenues, with the tax amnesty and moratorium already approved in the Senate representing another 0.2% of GDP.

We also watch for the fate of the new pension formula, which was approved by the Lower House and is pending discussion in the Senate. That formula, among other changes, includes an additional 7.2% increase in pensions to compensate for January’s real loss, carrying a fiscal cost of about 0.4% of GDP this year.This could increase up to 1.8% of GDP in the years to come, on our estimates, if real wages return to the levels seen in 2017 on average (note that the formula mandates an annual benefit increase equivalent to half the difference between the growth in nominal wages and annual inflation, if wages increase more than inflation). As spending contraction eases, and while waiting for economic activity to recover, securing additional revenues will become a necessary condition for the fiscal consolidation program to succeed.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

Chile: The CBC shaved the policy rate by 25bp, to 5.75%, as expected

The Board added that the policy rate has already accumulated the bulk of cuts planned for this year

We revise year-end policy rate level 25bp higher, to 5.5%, converging to 4.75% by Dec’25

Peru: The BCRP maintained the policy rate stable at 5.75%, keeping dependency mode ahead

The monetary authority has reiterated it will continue to cut the policy rate, independently of the Fed

## Chile: A cautious 25bp rate cut

In line with expectations, the CBC shaved the policy rate by 25bp, to 5.75%. Different from prior meetings, the decision was by majority as one director voted for a 50bp cut. As important, the cut was accompanied by a tweak to forward guidance. While the Board maintains the policy rate is to continue falling in the coming 2 years (policy horizon), they added the policy rate has already accumulated the bulk of the cuts planned for this year.

In the statement, the Board stresses the macroeconomic scenario has evolved as expected, although domestic demand in 1Q grew somewhat more than anticipated. The economy has been returning to a growth path consistent with its trend, with inflation on an annual basis converging to 3.4% by May, and 2-year inflation expectations remaining at 3%. Relative to March, the new developments in the central projection scenario are the better starting point for domestic demand, supported by higher copper prices, and the adjustment of electricity tariffs, which will significantly impact inflation, particularly in 2025. On the external side, the discussion on the Fed occupied an unusual amount of space, which makes sense given the acknowledged increased sensitivity of the exchange rate to interest rate differentials.

Following the decision, the CBC published the 2Q24 Monetary Policy Report (MPR), with revisions to the central macroeconomic scenario and forward guidance (see note). Consistent with the statement, forward guidance was also revised, for higher rate levels ahead. When the corridor midpoints are analyzed, and bearing in mind the levels correspond to quarterly averages, the correction is for 30bp higher for 4Q24, 50bp for 2Q25 and up to 61bp for 4Q25. The midpoint converges to 4.25% by the policy horizon (2Q26). When compared with our base case, we project both headline and core inflation above those revised in BCCh’s central scenario levels, while growth is aligned. Yet, as discussed yesterday, we also put relevance on the interest rate differential as a restriction to the speed of convergence of the policy rate lower. Given the expected acceleration of inflation above the target range ceiling, taking risk of adding FX pass-through would prove very risky when it comes to the risk of deanchoring inflation expectations. We thus revise the end-2024 level for the policy rate 25bp higher than our prior projection, to 5.5%. Ahead, the policy rate is expected to converge to 4.75% by Dec’25 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Chile nominal and real monetary policy rate %, deflating nominal MPR with 12m ahead inflation per CBC Survey and JPM
![](images/70118f907203ec366e8891a133a4cf2022ad7c0a21430773a755da04e1ce9190.jpg)

## Peru: The BCRP stood pat

The BCRP maintained the policy rate stable at 5.75%, as well as keeping data dependency ahead, with actions conditioned on new information about inflation and its determinants.

The statement noted the performance of inflation in May, with headline CPI down by -0.1% and core up by 0.2%. Thus, the 12-month inflation rate decreased to 2.0% in May, at the center of the target range, while core inched higher to 3.1%. Core CPI is showing some persistence associated with certain service sectors. On inflation expectations, next 12-month printed 2.6% in May, remaining within the target inflation range. Annual inflation is projected to remain around the center of the target range in the projection horizon. On activity, most economic activity expectation indicators are in the optimistic range.

Regarding the monetary path ahead, the monetary authority has reiterated it will continue to cut the policy rate, independently of the Fed decision. A narrower, or negative, interest rate differential may cause periods of downside pressure on the currency, but the monetary authority has reiterated its willingness to intervene, if need be. Of note, the BCRP has an outstanding position for PEN53.7bn (equivalent to US\$14.3bn) in FX swaps. In our central scenario, the policy rate converges to 5.0% by year-end, and terminal level sits at 4.25% by mid-2025 (Figure 2).

## United Kingdom

• No rate change from the BoE, but dovish language points to August cut

• BoE continues to downplay recent strength to argue persistence is receding

• Services inflation surprise in May was narrow

The BoE kept rates on hold this week, but some dovish elements of the statement suggest it is heading for an August rate cut provided there are no further upside inflation surprises. The upside surprise in the May CPI services print this week was quite narrow, with most of the subcomponents well behaved. Although underlying inflation remains strong, the BoE has a clear bias to cut and with limited data before the next meeting, we continue to anticipate an August easing. Growth news this week was mixed, with a strong retail sales gain and rising consumer confidence highlighting some upside risk around 2Q GDP, but the June PMI fell, and hints at a slowdown into 3Q. Meanwhile, opinion polls suggest Labour is on track for a very significant majority and attention is turning to how it will boost growth given the fiscal constraints it faces.

## Inflation at target, at least for now

UK headline CPI inflation for May fell from 2.3% to 2.0%oya (JPMorgan 2.0; Consensus 2.0; BoE 1.9) with core down from 3.9% to 3.5% (JPMorgan 3.4; Consensus 3.5). Services slowed from 5.9% to 5.7% (JPMorgan 5.5; Consensus 5.5; BoE 5.3). RPI was down from 3.3% to 3.0% (JPMorgan 3.0; Consensus 3.1). This out-turn was close to expectations, with the notable exception of services.

Based on our seasonal adjustment, we estimate that headline CPI prices rose 0.2%m/m in May, with core up 0.3%. Most categories look well behaved, with several surprising to the downside relative to our forecast. One example is core goods, where prices fell 0.1% over the month. Declines were broad based apart from medical products. Services rose 0.5% (almost 0.6% to 2.d.p), but this stemmed narrowly from a large 6% gain in transport services. This included a 14.9% rise in airfares, which is a volatile component. On this occasion it also included large gains in railway, road and sea fares. The transport services category in total added around 0.3%-pts to May services inflation. Excluding this, services prices rose by a more modest 0.2-0.3%m/m in May, with core prices up by just 0.1%. This is a big step down from April.

The persistence-weighted measure of underlying services inflation the MPC (used to) refer to did not show a large spike in May as it excludes airfares (Figure 1). That said, it did still come in at a firm 0.5% over the month using our seasonal

adjustment, and is running at a very strong 6.3% 3m/3m annualised pace (the oya was down from 6.0% to 5.8%). The acceleration in the 3m/3m run rate has a lot to do with April, while the monthly print for May was supported by ongoing strength in private rents and non-airfare parts of transport services that are not excluded (rail, road and sea).

## Pay settlements edging lower

Brightmine (formerly XpertHR) pay settlements data for the three months to May show a small rise in the median from 4.5% to 4.6% (Figure 2). However, the prior data through to April were revised down from 4.9% and so these data still show a moderating trend overall. This is consistent with the forward-looking part of the DMP survey. Mean settlements from Brightmine through to May showed a rise from 4.9% to 5.1%. This is higher than the median and seems more consistent with the BoE’s Agents survey, which pointed to 2024 pay growth of 5.4%, but out-turns here have slowed from the 6% rate recorded late year, are lower than the AWE data, and appear to be on the lower side of expectations from the Agents Survey.

## BoE inches towards August cut

The BoE left rates unchanged at 5.25% at its June meeting, with the vote remaining at 7-2 and broadly the same guidance as before. However, the wording of the statement and description of the recent data sound dovish, and we think this is a

signal that the internal centre ground of the committee is preparing a shift which would likely produce a majority in favour of a cut at the next meeting. This raises confidence in our long-standing call for an August cut.

The statement sounded quite dismissive of the recent upside surprises in core elements of the domestic wage and price data, even as the BoE staff revised up their 2Q GDP growth forecast from 0.2% to 0.5%q/q. The April CPI and AWE data were interpreted as one-off spikes related to indexation, regulated prices, the national living wage increase and “volatile components.” This is quite a strong statement given how broad-based and far above the MPC’s forecast the April surprise was. However, following this week’s May CPI data there was clearer evidence that most components looked quite well behaved despite another surprise in the aggregate services print.

The MPC’s statement concluded that “inflation persistence continued to moderate.” This still seems more like a forecast than a characterisation of where the data currently are, but nevertheless is a demonstration of the MPC’s bias to start easing. There was also a direct reference to the “August forecast round” in the statement as a chance to take stock of recent information and consider “how this affects the assessment that the risks from inflation persistence are receding”. The latter of the two quotes is unchanged from before, and on the surface it is quite natural to highlight August for the next evaluation, but in light of the broad conclusion reached in June that inflation persistence is indeed still moderating, it does sound quite leading as a hint about the next meeting.

Among the seven that didn’t vote for a cut, views are split into a camp that isn’t ready to go and one that sounds like it is on the verge of doing so. But the views of the more dovish camp - who in particular argued that the April inflation surprise was isolated, and hadn’t changed their medium-term outlook - seem to have more prominence in the committee’s summary statement. We take that as a hint that several key internal members have confidence they will vote for a cut in August and generate a majority. This group described their vote at this meeting as “finely balanced” despite large data surprises which appear to have prevented them easing in June.

This more dovish group among the seven probably includes Breeden, Bailey and Broadbent. This was Broadbent’s last meeting on the MPC, and he will be replaced by Lombardelli. Her views are not known, which introduces more uncertainty, but Huw Pill could be on board too, and even if not our guess would be that Lombardelli sides with Bailey. Its unusual for the Governor to be outvoted, let alone by a new internal member at their first meeting.

Despite its willingness to downplay ongoing strength in the recent data, the MPC does retain a degree of data dependency, but there would probably need to be further material surprises over the next six weeks to talk them out of a cut in August. As we have argued recently, survey pricing data are moving in a sympathetic way for a cut and those surveys were again referenced in the minutes of the June meeting.

## Mixed signs on growth

The Flash PMI for June took another step down from 53.0 to 51.7, the lowest level since last November. The mapping of the PMI into GDP has not been good lately, but we would usually equate this level with growth of 1.0-1.5% (Figure 3). Our forecast does anticipate a slowing in growth to 1% in 3Q, and so from this perspective the weaker PMI is not a total surprise, but we have been arguing there is upside risk to the forecast and the PMI tempers that bias a little.

Figure 3: PMI composite output and reported GDP growth
![](images/d6c0778e08b0dcc004f47fce51172925c3eba18cd4be6cd081d11fb0f5eb7bcf.jpg)

The orders and employment readings held broadly steady, with future output dropping by almost four points to 69.3, but the pricing components picked up, with output prices rising 1.5%-pts to 56.5, the highest since February. The MPC has recently been placing weight on this reading as evidence of moderating inflation persistence. The rise in pricing came from services despite the slowing in output being focused within that sector. One clear positive is manufacturing output, which has risen from the ashes to a lofty 54.2.

In contrast to the PMI’s message of growth moderation, retail sales rebounded from last month’s weather-related decline with a 2.9%m/m, sa gain in May. A recovery had looked likely but the magnitude was larger than expected. In isolation, this is worth around 0.1-0.2% on GDP for the month, and adds a little upside risk around our 2.0%q/q annualised forecast for 2Q GDP (which is now in line with the BoE’s forecast following the latest upward revision from Bank staff yesterday). The June Gfk consumer confidence survey showed a large three point gain to -14, the highest since September 2021. The recovery in major purchases has been a lot slower, and yesterday’s MPC minutes note that this is evidence of

monetary policy making households sceptical of spending on large items. Major purchases remain low but rose three points to -23 in June. We think there is upside here with household real income growth generally remaining firm. Unemployment fears continue to fall, and are also now at a late-2021 low.

## Emerging Europe

• Hungary: NBH slows the easing pace

• Poland: IP drops in May after big April gains

• Poland: Wage growth lingers in double digits

## Hungary: NBH slows the easing pace

This week the NBH decided to cut the base rate by 25bps to 7% (Figure 1), as expected, slowing the pace of easing further, from 50bps moves in recent meetings. The press release discloses a small portion of the new macro projections to be published in the inflation report. The growth outlook is exactly the same as in the March projection, with the economy expanding 2.5% in 2024 and then accelerating to 4% in 2025.

Figure 1: NBH interest rate corridor
![](images/149674a1d3c5bd9040c21f6efdd0e5ffbec961c5fbe5d05315d0e3cb3c273fb0.jpg)

The statement argues that the inflation outlook has improved, with recent CPI readings coming below expectations, due to energy in particular. This, together with improvements in Hungary’s risk profile are the key reasoning behind the decision to cut rates. However, the Council sees heightened financial market volatility, geopolitical risks and upside risks to inflation mandate a cautious approach and hence slowed the pace of cuts, reducing the base rate by just 25bps to 7%.

Deputy Governor Virag’s press conference kept the usual hawkish flair, highlighting upward-drifting core CPI more than benign headline, highlighting it will decide on a month to month basis, suggesting the room for further easing is “narrow” due to external risks, and indicating a pause will also be an option to consider. Overall the NBH’s decision was well received by the market. The 25bp cut was expected and the usual hawkish wrapping was successful in anchoring FX. For all its hawkishness, the message leaves the door for further cautious easing, and we think, with CPI likely within the target band in coming months (and through most of the year - Figure 2), the NBH will continue in small 25bp steps in July, all the way to 6.25%, which is our forecasted year-end rate. The biggest risk is FX, which remains extremely volatile and which could push the NBH to pause in a sell-off scenario.

![](images/2c207d963059147337ae8f864b2832504eee9d1f4000e3877e15b9fca24ff27d.jpg)

## Poland: May IP down, wage growth lingers

Industrial output fell 2.1%m/m sa in May (Figure 3), after a sequence of outsized moves in both directions (7%m/m in April, -5.2% in March). Through the noise, output is roughly where it was in February and is down 1.4% on a 3m/3m basis. However the base from 1Q24 is such that 2Q as a whole will most likely end up positive. We assume a 1%m/m sa recovery in June IP, which would leave the quarter up 2.1% vs 1Q, but even if growth is lower or negative, the quarterly figure will still be positive (it takes a drop of at least 5.4% in June to make the quarterly number negative). We lack details in seasonally adjusted terms for now, but the dynamics up to April were clear: the auto sector which had been the clear outperformer since early 2022, peaked in December 2023 and has since been in decline, dragging wider IP lower. Other manufacturing was extremely strong in 2020 and 2021, but has since struggled to grow.

Figure 3: Polish industrial production
![](images/a479634b9244138c426e050f45e0e874fc356bff562888d464c4da5e3a24ef52.jpg)

Nominal wage growth was roughly flat in May at 11.4%oya, from 11.3% in April (Figure 4), which implies real wages still expanding at 8.5%oya, also flat from April. At the same time private nonagricultural employment fell 0.2%m/m in May, usually a weak month. In seasonally adjusted terms employment is roughly flat (-0.04%m/m). This extends a trend of subtle weakness since early 2023, over the last 12 month 30 thousand jobs were shed which is a decline of 0.5% in the number of total private (nonagricultural) employment.

Figure 4: Private sector wage growth
![](images/d52dcea68c731a31bcbd53b0192e055493065a4c25e270a0659b423e3e13a3eb.jpg)

Czech Republic:

<table><tr><td rowspan="2">Fri Jun 28 9:00am</td><td colspan="5">Real GDP, final</td></tr><tr><td>%oya unless otherwise stated</td><td>2Q23</td><td>3Q23</td><td>4Q23</td><td>1Q24</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="5"></td><td>Real GDP,</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.6</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>swda %q/q saar</td><td>1.0</td><td>-3.1</td><td>1.8</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Private con- sumption</td><td>-4.4</td><td>-2.5</td><td>-0.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>GFCF</td><td>3.8</td><td>4.9</td><td>6.7</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr></table>

CNB likely to slow pace of easing with 25bp cut, in yet another close decision. Although the last CPI print brought some positive developments with it, the entire body of macro data over the past few months still shows inflation stuck above target, extremely high services CPI, consumption picking up and wage growth surprising to the upside. Therefore we think it would make sense for the CNB to slow the pace of easing next week, cutting by 25bps to 5%. However, public interventions by several members of the board, Governor Michl included, suggest it’s not so clear cut, as they all frame the discussion as between cutting 25bps or 50bps without hinting at a preference. After these statements markets priced approximately 40bps of cuts for next week (also weighing on the currency, which reversed some earlier gains). The board has maintained a cautious stance so far, and when in doubt has taken the less risky path, and we expect it will do the same this time with a 25bp cut, although it may be a split decision.

## South Africa

• Headline inflation was unchanged at 5.2%oya in May

• We expect CPI to average 5% in ‘24 and 4.7% in ’25

• We project the SARB to cut by 25bp in Nov, but now see slim chances of a September move

Headline inflation remained flat at 5.2%oya in May in line with market expectations, with non-core inflation remaining well behaved (Figure 1). Food prices rose 0.2%m/m, but eased a tenth to 4.3%oya (JPMe: 4.8%) mainly due to base effects from bread and cereal inflation. Yet, bread and cereal inflation momentum picked up to 0.8%m/m (-0.1%), consistent with the signal from the agricultural inputs. Meat, fish, fruits and vegetables experienced mild increases in the month. We expect monthly food prices to increase moderately-taking food inflation to 5.6%oya in August, before downshifting to around 4% in by December. Transport inflation rose to 6.3%oya last month (from 5.7%) on the back of a fuel price hikes, although the fuel tracker suggests more petrol price cuts are in store amid the recent USD/ZAR rally.

Figure 1: South Africa inflation outlook
![](images/1beaa6bb621eca1eae2530dd71cdfc538298ec4fd3cc3ed22bc30e69dbcca967.jpg)
Source: StatsSA, J.P. Morgan forecast

Services inflation – which has driven core dynamics in recent months – ticked up a tenth to 4.7%. Restaurants and hotels recorded gains of 0.8%m/m last month. The services inflation trajectory nonetheless remains unclear with medical insurance products (a major core CPI component) dynamics likely to remain relevant. Our inflation forecast is for core inflation to resume a downward drift to 4.3% in August, with core goods inflation likely contained. A case in point is vehicle inflation, which dropped further to 5.4%oya in May (from 5.5% in April, and 7% in March) and we see scope for further easing. Meanwhile, an unexpected rise in alcoholic beverages drove the miss relative to our core inflation forecast for a tenth drop to 4.5%oya (vs 4.6% actual) in May.

We expect headline inflation to remain contained in the nearterm and probably slow to 4.7%oya by end 2024. We expect the impact on headline inflation from a gentle increase in food costs to be offset by lower transport inflation. As a

result, we see a drop in headline inflation to the middle of the inflation target range by end 4Q, and an average 5%oya this year. That said, while the inflation developments are broadly dovish for the policy rate outlook, global factors remain relevant. Our base case is for a first policy rate cut in November, with about a 30% chance of a September move.

## Australia and New Zealand

The RBA remained on hold in the June meeting, but the language was more hawkish

• We expect that Australia’s monthly CPI indicator rose two-tenths to 3.8%oya in May

• NZ real GDP increased 0.2%q/q in 1Q24

The RBA kept the cash rate steady at 4.35% (IOER at 4.25%) this week, and the commentary shifted in a hawkish direction. The last line of the statement – "the board remains resolute in its determination to return inflation to target" – added back the clause "and will do what is necessary to achieve that outcome", having dropped the latter remark at the last two meetings. This suggests some creeping impatience. It will be "some time yet before inflation is sustainably in the target range", though there will be some optical support from subsidies mechanically dragging on inflation later in the year, which offers some short-term breathing room.

As discussed in our preview, the May meeting and minutes had put a lot of weight on consumption weakness as a signal that policy is restrictive. Last statement, the board noted households have been "curbing discretionary spending and maintaining their saving". Neither of those claims are quite as true now with the 1Q GDP data in hand, and so the board firmed up its language on households this week, stating "the revisions to consumption and the saving rate and the persistence of inflation suggest that risks to the upside remain."

We have argued that the tension between subsidy-lowered inflation and stronger real consumption/domestic demand through 2024 will keep the RBA in an ambiguous space, and prolong the board's uncertainty. Discussion of May's fiscal news had a similar flavour, as "recent budget outcomes may also have an impact on demand, although federal and state energy rebates will temporarily reduce headline inflation."

In that context, the discussion on inflation was naturally quite mixed. The data to hand show a stall in progress: "the pace of decline has slowed in the most recent data", and "inflation is easing but has been doing so more slowly than previously expected". Subsidies should depress 2H24 inflation, but those should lapse in 2025, which potentially compromises a full return to the target midpoint. In discussing these cross-currents, the overall perception is still of "excess demand in the economy", which should bias the board to look through shortterm subsidy drags.

## Hikes discussed at June RBA meeting

In the first question of the post meeting press conference, Governor Bullock conceded that a hike was discussed, and it was later revealed that a cut was never put on the table. In the end, the board decided to “stay the course”, which reflected a judgement that the reduced saving flows in the GDP data were not reflecting pure exuberance, but also a signal of households having limited room to flex to meet cost of living challenges.

If the level of consumption required to maintain current household commitments is above that required to bring inflation down, that would be a fairly dangerous state of affairs. We wouldn’t jump to the same conclusion as consumption has sequentially picked up, and real disposable income has further gains ahead. But taken alone, this interpretation is at least consistent with Bullock’s statement that the “narrow path” has become a bit narrower.

## Annual inflation to lift in May

We expect Australia’s monthly CPI indicator for May to show a slight pullback in prices, with a fall of 0.2%m/m nsa. The annual rate should rise from 3.6%oya to 3.8%oya, before easing again in June. April’s report had been firm (+0.7%m/m), driven by some short-term supply difficulties with some fruit items, and an upside surprise in home maintenance/repairs. Updates on services components were otherwise relatively scarce in the first month of the quarter, which the RBA referenced at this week’s decision as adding to their uncertainty about inflation momentum.

Figure 1: Australian textile inflation
![](images/4f363297a1d64c859e78856f2828f7c7570685156c85e3f1e242e62e57c41bb5.jpg)

This appeared to set up the reaction function for greater sensitivity on the upcoming data, though Bullock was at pains to say in the press conference that the odds of a hike haven’t gone up. In any case, May’s significant negative seasonality should prosaically help to trim perceptions of sticky inflation to some extent, with drags likely in the recreation and culture group (particularly holiday travel/accommodation), as well as clothing/footwear, and household furnishings. Clothing had been particularly perky in April (Figure 1) and we expect

some payback in May on the usual early Winter discounting/ restocking dynamic.

We also look for a deceleration in food prices, and in alcohol/ tobacco. The housing group should reaccelerate as the prior month’s reading was depressed by widening of eligibility for rental assistance, a drag which falls out again in this report. The education group and health groups are typically not updated in May and so we look for flat readings there, a return to growth in the finance/insurance group, and slightly steeper declines in the communications group approaching midyear. If our monthly forecast is realized the quarter will be on track for 0.8-0.9%q/q on headline, a little below the RBA’s implied SoMP forecast of 1.0%q/q.

## NZ GDP still weak

New Zealand’s 1Q GDP result was soft at +0.2%q/q sa. This is in line with the RBNZ’s forecast from the May MPS, above the consensus forecast of +0.1%q/q, but below ours at +0.4%q/q.

We had expected a return to modest growth in the March quarter, mostly on the back of exports, while maintaining the weak trend in domestic demand (which looks worse again in 2Q on the basis of the recent PMIs). In our preview we also noted the prospect of some modest upward revisions to GDP to better align with labor supply growth, and potential changes to how spending is allocated among visitors and domestic households.

The latter mix has been harder to ascertain given changes in travel flows in recent years, which have an outsized impact in March quarters when seasonality of tourism has historically been strongest. These factors played out as Stats NZ revised up 2023 slightly on net and reported a skewed exports/consumption mix noting it is "more challenging than usual to estimate quarterly seasonally annualized growth in certain series including, notably, household consumption expenditure". This estimation process is now under review.

Figure 2: GDP and composite PMI
![](images/dba364fa9ebdcf69cec2a688b1a16cf99a89472770ce5356ba449920511b4f25.jpg)
Source: Stats NZ, BNZ, J.P. Morgan

Even allowing for some degree of measurement noise, the picture of the domestic economy remains one of significant underperformance in GDP in comparison to labor supply growth (GDP: +0.3%oya vs working age population growth: +3.1%oya). The PMIs over the last week also suggest momentum faded again into 2Q (Figure 2).

## Greater China

• China: May activity data shows improving retail sales; FAI eases on housing drag

• PBOC kept one-year MLF rate unchanged

• Housing price decline widened further

• Taiwan: Further upside surprise in May export orders

• Next week: China inflation and credit, Taiwan CBC

China’s year-to-date monthly dataflow has been volatile. Following stronger-than-expected Jan-Feb activity, economic momentum moderated in March and April. The May activity data send mixed signals, with steady export activity and a modest upside surprise in retail sales. But FAI growth disappointed somewhat amid the ongoing housing market drag.

On industrial production, May IP came in moderately below expectations, growing 5.6%oya, or rising 0.4% m/m sa by our estimate, following the decline of 0.4% m/m sa in April. Domestic consumption demand seems to have improved modestly, following disappointments in recent months. Retail sales grew 3.7%oya, or 1.5% m/m sa in May. Service production growth also picked up to 4.8%oya (vs. 3.5% in April). Fixed investment came in modestly below expectations, rising 4.0%oya in Jan-May. The details show further deceleration in real estate investment (which fell 11.0%oya in May), with ongoing outperformance in manufacturing investment (+9.4%) and moderate easing in infrastructure investment growth (+4.9%) (Figure 1).

Figure 1: FAI growth by industry
![](images/a30d0d89d2412df82b3857a81801d152945cf35c02c2cb06aca13c4547e5aa5f.jpg)

Our forecast is for 2Q GDP growth to ease to 3.5% q/q saar (from 7.4% in 1Q). Our 2H forecast is for steady, trendgrowth pace of around 4.5%. For the full-year 2024, our growth forecast stands at 5.2%yoy. Regarding the risk factors, we highlight (1) weaker-than-expected housing activity and real estate investment, in part offset by solid manufacturing investment. Full-year real estate FAI will likely come in weaker than expected, possibly falling by about 9.5%oya based on the May data (previous forecast: 6-7% decline); (2)

sustainability of consumption and service recovery (two-sided risks, depending on policy measures); (3) export outlook, especially tariff increases (by the US and the EU recently) and increasing tensions from China’s price competition strategy (excess supply cited by the US) and geopolitical risks; and (4) policy implementation in the coming months, especially whether monetary policy will continue to stay cautious.

The return of a growth-supportive policy stance since the April politburo meeting could help to reduce downside risks in the near term. Nonetheless, we believe that policy transmission will take time. More importantly, we believe that directional changes in economic policy, especially removing the policy bias in favor of supply / production over boosting domestic demand, equal support for manufacturing upgrades and service upgrades, and equal support for SOE and non-SOE sectors, are critical to address various structural problems faced by the Chinese economy.

## PBOC kept policy rate unchanged

The PBOC kept the one-year MLF policy rate unchanged at 2.5% this week. The rate decision is not surprising, but, in our view, is disappointing. The official guidance on policy rates has been mixed recently. On April 30, the politburo meeting explicitly guided that the PBOC should use rate and RRR cuts to increase the support for the real economy, and the fiscal authority should accelerate the pace of government bond issuance. However, in the 1Q24 Monetary Policy Operation Report, the PBOC argued the slowdown in credit growth did not mean weaker support for the real economy, instead it was a result of a changing landscape of financial support (from indirect financing to direct financing) for economic transformation as well the an effort to improve credit efficiency.

We think the PBOC’s policy rate decision was constrained by two factors: net interest margin (NIM) compression pressure on the domestic front, and uncertainty in the Fed rate outlook and, by extension, pressure on capital flows and CNY depreciation pressure. Still, we think the PBOC’s rate cut will be more a question of timing, not a question of whether it will happen or not. The low inflation environment, high real interest rate for corporates and weak domestic demand jointly justify a growth-friendly macro policy stance, including rate cuts from the PBOC.

Our monetary policy forecast is revised to one 10bp rate cut in 3Q this year (previously: two 10bp rate cuts this year in 2Q and 4Q, respectively). We maintain our forecast of a 25bp RRR cut in 3Q (Figure 2). Meanwhile, the TSF growth forecast is revised down to 8.5% (previously: 9.0%) and the bank loan growth forecast is now 9.0% (previously: 9.5%). This mainly reflects the PBOC’s efforts to address the idle credit phenomenon, in particular, to ban make-up lending to corporates and in return offer a higher deposit rate, which is an important reason behind the slowdown in money supply growth (particularly M1 growth) and bank loan growth in recent months.

Figure 2: Policy rates and RRR in China
![](images/3e647e49355877da5d2fe816a6a649111d216588f02d615f9b7f3dce5ce410da.jpg)

## Housing price decline widened further

The decline in the NBS 70-city home prices widened in both %oya and %m/m terms, down 4.3%oya or 0.7%m/m nsa for new home prices, and down 6.8%oya or 0.9%m/m nsa for secondary home prices. Out of the NBS 70-city sample, Shanghai was the only city with (marginal) price increases in both %oya and %m/m terms in May. As of May, the 70 major cities’ new home prices declined 6.5% compared to the peak in August 2021, along with a 12.4% drop for secondary home prices (Figure 3).

Figure 3: China 70-city housing prices
![](images/fed6e2635aa6fbdbe37122aea365a99b7f71b2b1c143f253363193d1f089ee14.jpg)

It has been one month since the PBOC and NAFR announced three easing measures which aimed to increase house affordability. However, the policy impact has not materialized in the data yet. Residential home sales fell 19.0%oya (-17.1% in April) and -26.4% (vs. -29.2%) in volume and value terms in May. Most investors do not have much optimism for a meaningful home sales jump engineered by the recent policy easing. If home sales momentum disappoints in the coming months, market expectations may arise for further policy support. Other instruments in the government’s housing policy toolkit include easing of resale restrictions, exemption or

reduction of value-added tax and transaction taxes.

We were looking for around 10% decline in major housing indicators for the full-year, including floor space started and floor space sold, and a 6-7% decline in real estate FAI. However, year-to-date tracking points to some downside risk. We revise down our full-year land sales forecast from -10%oya to -15%, and new starts from -10% to -14%. For home sales, the base effect will turn more favorable in 2H. However, a 10% decline is still challenging for the full year and we have tweaked the forecast to -13%. For real estate FAI, we see a higher probability that the offset from public housing (e.g., urban village renovation related construction activity) may not play out in a timely manner this year. Hence, we further revise down our full-year real estate FAI forecast to around 9.5% decline (previously: a decline of 6-7%).

## Hong Kong: Unemployment rate at 3.0%

Hong Kong’s unemployment rate stayed at 3.0% sa for the three months ending May (Figure 4). Total employment rose 2.8k to 3,696.9k, while the labor force rose 5.3k to 3,813.1k. Seasonally adjusted, total employment rose 0.2%m/m sa, while the labor force rose 0.1%m/m sa. It is encouraging that both total employment and labor force have been rising (after our seasonal adjustment) for four consecutive months.

Figure 4: Hong Kong unemployment rate
![](images/b473c5414b78677df4614df9f1d8fdada3c4a632d94e9bdbaf43054942c84829.jpg)

Business indicators remained weak in May. The SME business receipt current diffusion index (DI) fell 0.8pt to 43.0. Total economy PMI fell 1.4pt to 49.2, while the employment component of PMI fell 1.7pt to 49.5. The imbalance in crossborder travel activities continued in the first 19 days of June, weighing on the further recovery of consumption and tourism-related sectors. Overall, the city’s unemployment rate has returned to the lowest level since the onset of the pandemic, and we expect it to remain around 3% in the near term.

## CPI rose a modest 1.2%oya

Hong Kong’s headline CPI inflation came in line with our expectation at 1.2%oya in May, compared to +1.1%oya in April (Figure 5). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that headline CPI edged up 0.1%m/m sa in May (vs. -0.4%m/m sa in April). In the breakdown, food prices rose 0.3%m/m sa, with food excluding meals away from home rising 0.6%. Housingrelated prices fell 0.1%m/m sa, following the 1.2% decline in April. Prices of electricity, gas and water fell 1.7%m/m sa. Excluding food and energy, core CPI grew 1.5%oya (or +0.1m/m sa) in May. Netting out the effects of all one-off government relief measures, the composite CPI rose 1.0%oya in May (vs. 0.9%oya in April).

Figure 5: Hong Kong CPI
![](images/8ddfc57446715c766dbcd780f53cf36d76394fd19b90490d9aba96ac2c0ce0d6.jpg)
2013 2014 2015 2016 2017 2018 2019 2020 2021 2022 2023 2024 Source: HK C&SD, J.P. Morgan

CPI inflation has remained soft in the first five months of the year, growing 1.6%oya in Jan-May, with sequential trend (measured in 3m/3m saar terms) on the downturn in the near term. Considering the weakness in domestic consumption, and the ongoing imbalance between inbound and outbound travel activities, we revise down our full-year CPI inflation forecast to 1.4%y/y (previously: 2.1%y/y).

## Taiwan: Upside surprise in export orders

Taiwan’s May export orders came in above expectations again. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate export orders showed further solid gains of 1.9% m/m sa, with sequential trend growth strong at 40.3% 3m/3m saar. Orders by source suggest broad-based demand growth across major markets. Orders from the US (+34.2% 3m/3m saar in May) and Europe (+115.4%) have led overall orders growth in recent months, while orders from China/HKSAR have also picked up lately (+35.5%). Orders from the ASEAN region came off (-13.7%) from a high base.

Tech orders showed a solid increase of 2.9% m/m sa in May, with strong sequential trend growth at 72.0% 3m/3m saar (Figure 6), reflecting a broad-based upturn in orders for electronics products as well information and communication products. Non-tech orders unperformed somewhat, with modest growth at 1.8% 3m/3m saar in May. Meanwhile, machinery orders seem to have picked up lately, rising 13.5% 3m/3m saar in May, consistent with the constructive outlook on global capex.

Figure 6: Taiwan export orders
![](images/68eec3bad17ba317d44ed6b18188e2f5b9603940520bc1b28fe5aa752ff4ceaf.jpg)

Taiwan tech export orders have underperformed actual tech exports since early 2023, reflecting the boost of AI-related tech demand (largely produced in Taiwan manufacturing plants), while a significant share of export orders involves other traditional tech products manufactured offshore. The latest solid upturn in Taiwan’s tech export orders likely suggest broader-based demand improvement for overall tech products, including those manufactured from mainland China.

## Korea

• Customs exports hold up in June according to the flash data

• Producer price sequential disinflation continues in May

There were no major data releases this week. The flash customs exports data suggest that June nominal exports should hold up to imply a quarterly growth recovery in 2Q after a temporary pause in 1Q. The producer price index continued its gradual disinflationary trend in May. While the gradually stabilizing inflation trend is broadly in line with our and the BoK’s expectation, the high-frequency data does not suggest a sudden change in the GDP growth outlook to the downside. Net exports’ positive growth contribution should continue, and the domestic demand situation may not divert materially from the previous quarters’ trend. On this backdrop, we don’t expect a major change in the Bank of Korea’s policy signal in the upcoming July meeting: we expect a gradual normalization of policy stance to slowly neutralize the restrictive stance starting from 4Q this year.

## Customs exports to hold up in June

Customs exports rose 8.5%oya in the first 20 days of June. Adjusting for trading day differences, we estimate that the full-month exports should rise 1.5%oya in June. The fullmonth growth estimate based on the 20-day data is a bit lower than the noisier estimate based on the flash 10-day report (we had estimated a 4.0%oya gain based on the 10-day data). As a result, seasonally adjusted 20-day exports rose 0.7%m/m, sa in June (vs. the 8.5% gain in 10-day exports). That said, the modest 0.7% gain in June exports implies a resumption of robust nominal exports quarterly growth in 2Q, after a -0.8% annualized rate contraction in 1Q. The current tracking of three-month trend growth of customs exports is 10.7%3m/3m, saar (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Korea customs exports
![](images/39f3f58a39be06c7f5c79d987e89da23ba82f29bb51f9f1c5b1221265c16d583.jpg)

## Sequential disinflation in producer prices

The producer price index rose 0.2%m/m, sa, in May, after remaining flat in March and inching down by 0.1% in April. Sequentially, headline PPI inflation declined to 0.7% 3m/3m, saar, in May, after hovering around a mid-2% pace in the first three months of the year, continuing to slow from a 4.9% peak last October (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Producer price index
![](images/c95c318f7dc796f151a3b28a9610bb564b9e3837c616333e6a4c6841e817e125.jpg)
Source: BoK and J.P. Morgan

Core PPI rose 0.3%m/m in May after staying almost flat in the prior three months. Services prices rose strongly by 0.5% in May after a stable pace within the range of 0.0-0.2% over the past year. Prices of IT and communication services led the rise in overall services, rising 2.4% in May (vs. usual +/- 0.1% fluctuations), due to an increase in the standard rate for software development in public institutions, according to officials. Prices of non-oil industrial goods rose more modestly by 0.2% after marginally edging down by 0.04% in March and 0.02% in April. Sequentially, core PPI inflation moderated further to 0.7% 3m/3m, saar, in May, from low-2% prints in 1Q and a 3.4% peak in last November.

On the supply side, price pressure has broadly moderated. Energy PPI rose 0.3%m/m in May after falling for two months, mainly due to a hike in gas tariffs for industrial use during the month, while it should gradually reflect the stabilization of global oil prices going forward (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Producer prices - energy
![](images/55634e4a6843233b4fdfbfe4910ef2e140352d9a515dfc151ce8340f145c9881.jpg)
Farm product prices fell 4.5%m/m in May following a 0.5% drop in April, reversing an average 1.8% increase over the

prior three months, as local supply conditions for fresh food stabilized. The category was previously one of the major sources of upward pressure for about a year until 1Q, and the recent monthly falls brought down its sequential rate to 5.4%3m/3m, saar, in May from a 16.3% peak in April.

## ASEAN

• Vietnam’s IP recovered in April-May catching up with resilient exports

• Core prices continue to drive inflation stabilization sequentially

FX stability remains a top priority for the SBV, while both ends of interest rates are capped through OMO

Vietnam’s May data largely conformed with our view that the country’s manufacturing should recover this quarter after taking a breather in 1Q24, catching up with resilient external demand. Export details indicate a continuing boost from the tech cycle and a broadening base of external demand growth, which should help cushion the gradual slowdown of US demand. We continue to expect overall growth to normalize at a trend-like pace (6.0%y/y this year) on a buffer of solid exports, despite headwinds from domestic monetary tightening since April.

## IP has caught up with resilient exports

IP rose 2.6%m/m, sa, in May, on top of April’s 2.3% gain, picking up its sequential growth to 10.3%3m/3m, saar, after a quarter of correction. Customs exports also increased 4.9%m/ m in May, although their sequential growth dipped to a slightly negative 0.7%3m/3m, saar, as a correction from the robust 15-20%ar pace during 2H23-1Q24 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Vietnam IP and customs exports
![](images/33cf42bf30e3bb5da223407f0fd3dd7519006e4bc1973b003e4d817d67ab02db.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan

In the details, tech exports marked 14.8%3m/3m, saar, growth in May, remaining strong throughout the past year (in the range of 10-35%ar), suggesting relentless support from the tech sector’s upcycle. By destination, exports to the US marked -5.9%3m/3m, saar, in April (latest available data), as a correction after a year of strong growth (40-50%ar at their peak in 4Q23). But exports to Western Europe (+22.4%3m/3m, saar) and China (+27.1%3m/3m, saar, after five months of decline until February) provided a buffer, echoing the latest global PMI reports that hint at a narrowing of regional gaps (Figure 2).

![](images/356659c13001cf78ec0eef349f1c4c85b75418b1ad410d68b513ef819c90f9d1.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan

Domestic retail sales (CPI-deflated) rose 0.8%m/m, sa, in May, keeping the current pace in the past few months. Their sequential growth accelerated to 9.4%3m/3m, saar, from 0.9% in February, indicating that consumption is on the rise despite the tightening of domestic liquidity. The manufacturing sector’s strength could have spilled over to consumption through the income channel, and the continuing recovery in tourism inflows might have contributed as well (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Vietnam retail sales and int'l tourism receipts
![](images/28d819bac24575fd0d5883bd30dc43036bc806252571154af23662d8586dc0dd.jpg)

## Sequential disinflation continues

Headline CPI inflation stood at 4.4%oya in May, higher than 3.4% in January and 4.0% in February-March, due to alreadyanticipated base effects. Sequentially, headline moderated to 2.6%3m/3m, saar, and core stabilized at 2.1%-2.3% since the start of the year. We continue to expect core's gradual disinflation to determine the broad inflation path this year, and the central bank’s relatively generous inflation target of an annual average of 4%-4.5% will be likely achieved.

Food inflation was recently a bit stronger than we had assumed, rising 0.4%m/m for the third month in May (Figure 4), which could be related to heatwaves and drought affecting local agricultural supply. More recently the severe weather conditions have somewhat eased, as the country enters the rainy season in late-May. Thus, if we are correct in attributing the recent strong food prices to the weather, then it should abate going forward.

Figure 4: Vietnam CPI inflation
![](images/32d1b430a4d62a1a742649cf13e50d0c3ea37e1f21c6887c9d98e2a6026b7254.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan

## FX stability remains top priority for SBV

FX stability has been a key concern in recent months, and the SBV has stepped up currency defense by selling its FX reserves to commercial banks (US\$3.0-3.5bn sold since its first intervention on April 19 in our estimates). More recently on May 24, the central bank reiterated its plan to keep selling dollars to some banks as a way of supporting the local currency and denounced speculation about possible changes in its FX trading band policy as inaccurate.

Not solely relying on FX reverses, the SBV also started a stealth monetary tightening by selling T-bills to set the lower bound to VNIBOR since March and raising their issuance rates consistently from 3.75% on April 26 to 4.00% on May 22 and 4.25% May 29. It also raised the reverse repo rate (that serves as the upper bound to VNIBOR) by 50bps to 4.50% on May 22 following a 50bp hike on April 23.

## Interest rates high, but not so high

That said, the central bank is also avoiding excessively high interest rates. Credit growth has slowed since the beginning of the year and marked 7.7%3m/3m, saar, in May (Figure 5), lower than the annual policy target of 14-15%. Thus, the SBV would like to maintain interest rates high enough to support the local currency but not so high to impede credit growth, juggling between its objectives of FX stability and credit growth facilitation. This intent is proved by its open market operations (OMO) conducted bi-directionally since April, which has constrained both ends of short-term interest rates. It will likely continue to rely on fine-tuning via daily OMO, instead of changing the refinancing rate that would signal a more profound shift in its policy stance.

Figure 5: Vietnam credit growth
![](images/fbff3d741607f835eaece59595dac298374d04fcd18d67ad774c1e0b0b12ed0f.jpg)
Source: SBV and J.P. Morgan

As a result of these efforts, the currency pair has been broadly stable since May, moving only narrowly around VND 25,450 per USD, the level which the central bank revealed its intention to defend with the sales of dollars. The short-term interbank interest rates also have been within the range of 4.25%-4.50% set by the bi-direction OMO over the past month (Figures 6 and 7).

Figure 6: USDVND exchange rate
![](images/6df4b02c8d74fb313da293644336c63e2b50ade42a8cc8f1266d462329d5ab28.jpg)

Figure 7: Vietnam money market rates and SBV OMO
![](images/657a961a67e0ac1e83e214359592547a5c9c7e9c829d31cbc2f7b94be66be27f.jpg)

We estimate the SBV held FX reserves of about US\$88bn by the end of this May, equivalent to 3.3 months of good imports. The central bank’s past practices keeping reserves above 3 months of imports suggests potential room to utilize up to around US\$7-8bn. It falls short of US\$5.0bn and US\$ 15.6bn spent back in 2Q and 3Q22, respectively, when the country last saw currency weakness. But a larger goods and services trade surplus this time should provide a buffer. Thus,

Merchandise trade (Jun 18)

US\$ bn, sa, 3m sum, both axes

we think the central bank has capability to maintain the current equilibrium of FX and rates for the next few months, waiting for a dovish pivot from the US Fed in 2H which may end a period of dollar strength.

Figure 8: Vietnam monthly proxy for BOP
![](images/ab818e463565817cebb83d510f7624f9bbf047cf63504628be9edcba723513f6.jpg)
Source: GSO and J.P. Morgan estimates

## India

• Government approves MSP price hike for Kharif season

• CPI-weighted MSP hiked by 5.6% vs 7% last year

IMD revises June rainfall forecast to below normal

• Trade deficit widens on stronger oil imports

The central government approved a 1.4-12.7% increase in the Minimum Support Prices (MSP) – the minimum price at which government guarantees to purchase – for 14 Kharif (summer) crops for the 2024-25. The CPI-weighted price increase was 5.6%, slightly below the 7% hike last year and only marginally above 4.5% average hike in the last five years (Figure 1 & Table 1). Amongst the major crops, rice prices increased by 5.4% vis-à-vis 7% last year and pulses prices were raised by 5.8 % compared to 7.1% last year.

The MSP prices typically act as a floor for market prices, particularly in rice and pulses, where governments procurement is meaningful. The new MSP prices are unlikely to push up market prices as they are still not above the current market prices. This augurs well for the inflation outlook. The increase in MSP is in line with the government policy of keeping MSPs at least 1.5 times above the cost of production. More broadly, the hike in MSP which is not far from the last few years is suggestive of policy continuity.

Figure 1: CPI weighted MSP
![](images/df10ee436f741e59269301155e9c4f5e947a2e9d3ca0f228e09612b4fce640f5.jpg)

## June rainfall revised to below normal

The Indian Meteorological Department’s revised forecast for June rainfall shows it is below normal, at less than 92% of the long-period average (LPA). As of June 19 the cumulative deficit for the season stands at 18% below the LPA, despite the monsoon reaching the mainland two days earlier than usual. The south peninsula has received rainfall 15% above LPA; the North-west and Central regions have so far lagged receiving 32% and 70% below normal rains. June only accounts for 19% of total cumulative rainfall for the June to September monsoon season. Meteorological officials are forecasting a strong revival of the monsoon in the coming days, with La Niña conditions expected to offer further support in the latter half of the season.

## Trade deficit widens in May

The merchandise trade deficit widened to \$23.8bn in May from \$19.1bn (nsa) in April driven by stronger imports. Notably, oil imports surged in May resulting in record high net oil imports of \$13.2 bn from \$9.8 bn in April (Figure 2). Trade deficit ex. oil widened more modestly to \$10.6 bn from \$9.3 bn in April. The imports continued to firm up with manufacturing imports (proxy for underlying volume) rising sequentially in May. The manufacturing exports growth continued to hold up in May helped by improvements in global goods sector.

Figure 2: Net oil imports
![](images/b671fdca68f4aece4554d49b84db10d95bbe81c5cfff14c0edaa4dba80bdd827.jpg)

Figure 3: Trade deficit ex oil
![](images/8847eeb3885faabe24e8dca11f6ae902c552a2e85bbeb6d38e2b194ddf7c7a3e.jpg)

## Net services exports soften further

Net services exports further eased to an 11-month low of \$12.8bn in May from \$13.7bn in April driven by an increase in services imports even as services exports remained largely similar (Figure 4). The increase in the trade deficit coupled with the easing of net services exports has resulted in a rise in combined (merchandise and services) deficit to \$10.9bn, highest since October, up from \$5.4 bn in April (Figure 5). The trends so far this quarter are likely to result in a sharp swing in current account balance (CAB) from a minor surplus in 1Q24 to a deficit of \~1.5% of GDP in 2Q24.

## Asia Focus: Food inflation

Food prices in Asia have been subject to a series of shocks over recent years, from severe weather disruptions to increased trade protectionism. Following the El Nino phenomenon last year and the extreme heatwave in the last few months, the UN World Meteorological Department is forecasting a 60-70% chance of La Nina in 2H24 (see link). La Nina is essentially the opposite of El Nino, where cooler sea surface temperatures lead to a spell of heavier-than-normal rainfall in affected regions.

Although the La Nina phenomenon could theoretically damage crop yields via floods, historically, it arrives on the back of El Nino episodes and brings much-needed precipitation for farmers. This may explain subdued food PPI even when the El Nino Southern Oscillation (ENSO) index shows high positive readings (i.e., La Nina), except when the Ukraine war disrupted food prices in 2022 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: ENSO index vs. EM Asia food PPI
![](images/c443b8ef7ad1f79fbbe2fd07ed9798d1a8d40ee11bdc580ce4e166b7de84635d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P., J.P. Morgan

Nonetheless, the strength of La Nina this time around could be diminished by the lingering effects of last year’s El Nino (e.g., extreme heat). For example, India’s monsoon has delivered 20% less rain so far this season (see link). As such, the drag on water-intensive agriculture output could persist, with ramifications on fresh food and overall food prices.

The impact of macro disruptions such as weather on overall food inflation also depends on micro forces at the country level. Among the most agriculturally driven Asian economies, our analysis shows that the Philippines has the highest food PPI to CPI pass-through coefficient (Table 1). Retail price sensitivity to wholesale prices has declined in Thailand but has remained steady for India.

Table 1: Food PPI to CPI passthrough coefficient¹
<table><tr><td>Time period</td><td>ID</td><td>PH</td><td>TH</td><td>IN</td><td>EM Asia²</td></tr><tr><td>2012-16</td><td>0.2</td><td>(3.7)</td><td>3.1</td><td>1.0</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>2017-21</td><td>(3.8)</td><td>(6.2)</td><td>1.2</td><td>1.0</td><td>(2.0)</td></tr><tr><td>2022-now</td><td>1.0</td><td>1.7</td><td>0.5</td><td>1.1</td><td>1.1</td></tr></table>

Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan estimates
1. CPI %chg over PPI % chg 2. Simple average of ID, PH, TH, IN

For the Philippines, the pass-through from food PPI to CPI is particularly efficient because of structural rice shortages, made worse by India’s export restrictions and profiteering behavior by private sector importers in the past year. However, recent supply-side responses (e.g., 5-year rice deal with Vietnam, import tariff cut from 35% to 15%) are potential game-changers that could stabilize rice prices (Figure 2).

1. Simple average of ID, PH, TH, and IN 2. Shaded region denotes last La Nina episode
Figure 2: Philippines rice prices
![](images/ca21784412bc83cd70701e6ff02d751f33b935d0fd3f81bc223fbea30a1ee1db.jpg)

In Thailand’s case, prepared food prices do not correlate with wholesale food prices because other overhead cost factors (e.g., labor, electricity, rent) are more important. Raw food CPI does tend to follow agricultural PPI (Figure 3) except in the past nine months, with excess supply in certain raw food items a possible explanation.

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
